Prompt

1. 样本数据6,8,4,5,12的中位数为（ ）
A. 5
B. 6
C. 8
D. 9

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
要计算一组数据的中位数，需按照以下步骤进行：

1. **排序**：将样本数据按从小到大的顺序排列。
   原数据：`6, 8, 4, 5, 12`
   排序后：`4, 5, 6, 8, 12`

2. **确定位置**：该组数据共有 `5` 个（奇数个）。对于奇数个数据，中位数就是排序后位于正中间的那个数，即第 `(5+1)/2 = 3` 个数。

3. **取值**：排序后的第3个数是 `6`，因此该组数据的中位数为 `6`。

对照选项，正确答案为 **B**。

\boxed{B}

gaokao_000_001 ✓ A A 1602 54.2 29.5 http://192.168.0.41:9292
Prompt
请解答以下数学题（单选题）。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

2. 已知平面向量\vec{a}，\vec{b}不共线，且2\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}，则（ ）
A. x=2, y=-3
B. x=-2, y=3
C. x=2, y=3
D. x=-2, y=-3

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
**逐步推理：**

1. **理解已知条件**：题目给出平面向量 $\vec{a}$ 与 $\vec{b}$ **不共线**。根据平面向量基本定理，任意两个不共线的向量可以作为该平面的一组基底，且平面内任一向量用这组基底线性表示时，其表示形式是**唯一**的。

2. **利用向量相等的充要条件**：若两个向量相等，且它们都是用同一组不共线基底线性表示的，则对应基底的系数必须分别相等。
   已知等式为：
   $$2\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}$$

3. **对比系数**：
   - 对比 $\vec{a}$ 的系数：左边为 $2$，右边为 $x$，故 $x = 2$。
   - 对比 $\vec{b}$ 的系数：左边为 $y$，右边为 $-3$，故 $y = -3$。

4. **得出结论**：解得 $x=2$，$y=-3$。对照选项，与选项 A 完全一致。

**最终答案：**
\boxed{A}
Reasoning
Here's a thinking process:

1.  **Analyze the User Input:**
   - **Question Type:** Multiple-choice question (单选题)
   - **Topic:** Plane vectors (平面向量)
   - **Given:** Vectors $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ are non-collinear (不共线). Equation: $2\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}$.
   - **Options:**
     - A. $x=2, y=-3$
     - B. $x=-2, y=3$
     - C. $x=2, y=3$
     - D. $x=-2, y=-3$
   - **Task:** Solve step-by-step and put the final answer in `\boxed{}`.

2.  **Identify Key Mathematical Concepts:**
   - Vector equality: If two vectors are equal, their corresponding coefficients with respect to a basis must be equal.
   - Basis vectors: Since $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ are non-collinear (不共线), they form a basis for the plane. This means any vector in the plane can be uniquely expressed as a linear combination of $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$.
   - Therefore, if $c_1 \vec{a} + c_2 \vec{b} = d_1 \vec{a} + d_2 \vec{b}$, then $c_1 = d_1$ and $c_2 = d_2$.

3.  **Apply Concepts to the Given Equation:**
   - Given: $2\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}$
   - Compare coefficients of $\vec{a}$: Left side has coefficient 2, right side has coefficient $x$. So, $x = 2$.
   - Compare coefficients of $\vec{b}$: Left side has coefficient $y$, right side has coefficient $-3$. So, $y = -3$.

4.  **Match with Options:**
   - Calculated: $x = 2$, $y = -3$
   - Option A: $x=2, y=-3$ -> Matches exactly.
   - Option B: $x=-2, y=3$ -> Incorrect.
   - Option C: $x=2, y=3$ -> Incorrect.
   - Option D: $x=-2, y=-3$ -> Incorrect.

5.  **Formulate Step-by-Step Reasoning:**
   - State the condition: $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ are non-collinear plane vectors, which means they are linearly independent and can serve as a basis.
   - Use the property of vector equality: For vectors expressed in the same basis, equality holds if and only if the coefficients of corresponding basis vectors are equal.
   - Set up equations: From $2\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}$, equate coefficients of $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$.
     - Coefficient of $\vec{a}$: $2 = x$
     - Coefficient of $\vec{b}$: $y = -3$
   - Conclude: $x=2, y=-3$.
   - Match with option A.
   - Put final answer in `\boxed{A}`.

6.  **Self-Correction/Verification:**
   - Check condition: "不共线" (non-collinear) ensures uniqueness of representation. If they were collinear, coefficients wouldn't necessarily match. So the condition is crucial and correctly applied.
   - Check algebra: $2=x$, $y=-3$. Correct.
   - Check options: A matches.
   - Format: Step-by-step reasoning followed by `\boxed{A}`. All good.

   Draft response:
   根据平面向量基本定理，若两个不共线的向量 $\vec{a}, \vec{b}$ 作为基底，则平面内任一向量用它们表示的形式是唯一的。
   已知等式：$2\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}$。
   由于 $\vec{a}$ 与 $\vec{b}$ 不共线，根据向量相等的充要条件，对应基底的系数必须分别相等。
   对比 $\vec{a}$ 的系数，得 $x = 2$；
   对比 $\vec{b}$ 的系数，得 $y = -3$。
   因此，$x=2, y=-3$，对应选项 A。
   最终答案：\boxed{A}

Prompt
请解答以下数学题（单选题）。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

3. 已知集合A={sin\frac{7\pi}{6}, cos\frac{5\pi}{3}, tan\frac{5\pi}{4}}, B={-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}, 1}，则A \cap B = （ ）
A. {-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}}
B. {-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, 1}
C. {-\frac{1}{2}, 1}
D. {-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}, 1}

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
**逐步推理：**

1. **化简集合 $A$ 中的三角函数值：**
   * $\sin\frac{7\pi}{6} = \sin\left(\pi + \frac{\pi}{6}\right) = -\sin\frac{\pi}{6} = -\frac{1}{2}$
   * $\cos\frac{5\pi}{3} = \cos\left(2\pi - \frac{\pi}{3}\right) = \cos\frac{\pi}{3} = \frac{1}{2}$
   * $\tan\frac{5\pi}{4} = \tan\left(\pi + \frac{\pi}{4}\right) = \tan\frac{\pi}{4} = 1$
   
   因此，集合 $A$ 化简为：$A = \left\{-\frac{1}{2}, \frac{1}{2}, 1\right\}$。

2. **列出集合 $B$：**
   已知 $B = \left\{-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}, 1\right\}$。

3. **求交集 $A \cap B$：**
   交集是指同时属于集合 $A$ 和集合 $B$ 的所有元素。对比两集合的元素：
   * $-\frac{1}{2}$ 同时在 $A$ 和 $B$ 中；
   * $1$ 同时在 $A$ 和 $B$ 中；
   * $\frac{1}{2}$ 仅在 $A$ 中；
   * $-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$ 仅在 $B$ 中。
   
   故公共元素为 $-\frac{1}{2}$ 和 $1$，即 $A \cap B = \left\{-\frac{1}{2}, 1\right\}$。

4. **对照选项：**
   选项 C 为 $\left\{-\frac{1}{2}, 1\right\}$，与计算结果完全一致。

\boxed{C}

Prompt
请解答以下数学题（单选题）。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

4. 曲线y=5x+8lnx在点(1,5)处的切线方程为（ ）
A. y=3x+2
B. y=5x
C. y=8x-3
D. y=13x-8

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
要求曲线 $y=5x+8\ln x$ 在点 $(1,5)$ 处的切线方程，可按以下步骤逐步推理：

1. **求函数的导数**：  
   对 $y=5x+8\ln x$ 求导，根据求导法则得：
   $$y' = (5x)' + (8\ln x)' = 5 + \frac{8}{x}$$

2. **计算切点处的斜率**：  
   切线的斜率等于函数在切点横坐标处的导数值。将 $x=1$ 代入导数：
   $$k = y'\big|_{x=1} = 5 + \frac{8}{1} = 13$$

3. **利用点斜式写出切线方程**：  
   已知切点为 $(x_0, y_0) = (1, 5)$，斜率 $k=13$，代入点斜式 $y - y_0 = k(x - x_0)$：
   $$y - 5 = 13(x - 1)$$

4. **化简为一般形式**：  
   $$y - 5 = 13x - 13$$
   $$y = 13x - 8$$

对比题目所给选项，该方程与选项 D 完全一致。

\boxed{D}

   $$8^2 = 2p_1 \times 4 \implies 64 = 8p_1 \implies p_1 = 8$$

gaokao_000_005 ✓ B B 4398 54.9 80.1 http://192.168.0.41:9292
Prompt
请解答以下数学题（单选题）。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

6. 已知函数f(x)=\frac{x+2}{e^x+a}的最大值为1，则a = （ ）
A. \frac{1}{2}
B. 1
C. \frac{3}{2}
D. 2

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
**逐步推理过程：**

1. **求函数的导数**
   对 $f(x)=\frac{x+2}{e^x+a}$ 使用商的求导法则：
   $$f'(x)=\frac{(x+2)'(e^x+a)-(x+2)(e^x+a)'}{(e^x+a)^2}=\frac{1\cdot(e^x+a)-(x+2)e^x}{(e^x+a)^2}=\frac{a-(x+1)e^x}{(e^x+a)^2}$$

2. **寻找极值点（驻点）**
   令 $f'(x)=0$，分母恒不为零（由选项知 $a>0$，故 $e^x+a>0$），只需分子为零：
   $$a-(x+1)e^x=0 \quad \Rightarrow \quad (x+1)e^x=a$$
   设该方程的解为 $x_0$。由于 $h(x)=(x+1)e^x$ 在 $x>-1$ 时单调递增且值域为 $(0,+\infty)$，故对任意 $a>0$，方程有唯一实根 $x_0$。当 $x<x_0$ 时 $f'(x)>0$，当 $x>x_0$ 时 $f'(x)<0$，因此 $x_0$ 是函数的唯一极大值点，也是全局最大值点。

3. **利用最大值条件列方程**
   已知最大值为 $1$，即 $f(x_0)=1$：
   $$\frac{x_0+2}{e^{x_0}+a}=1 \quad \Rightarrow \quad x_0+2=e^{x_0}+a$$
   将驻点条件 $a=(x_0+1)e^{x_0}$ 代入上式：
   $$x_0+2=e^{x_0}+(x_0+1)e^{x_0}=e^{x_0}(1+x_0+1)=(x_0+2)e^{x_0}$$

4. **求解参数 $a$**
   整理方程得：
   $$(x_0+2)e^{x_0}-(x_0+2)=0 \quad \Rightarrow \quad (x_0+2)(e^{x_0}-1)=0$$
   解得 $x_0=-2$ 或 $x_0=0$。
   - 若 $x_0=-2$，则 $f(-2)=\frac{0}{e^{-2}+a}=0\neq 1$，舍去。
   - 若 $x_0=0$，代入 $a=(x_0+1)e^{x_0}$ 得 $a=(0+1)e^0=1$。

5. **验证**
   当 $a=1$ 时，$f(x)=\frac{x+2}{e^x+1}$。由上述推导知 $x=0$ 为最大值点，且 $f(0)=\frac{2}{1+1}=1$，完全符合题意。

综上所述，$a=1$，对应选项 B。

\boxed{B}
Reasoning
Here's a thinking process:

1.  **Analyze the User Input:**
   - **Question:** Given the function $f(x) = \frac{x+2}{e^x + a}$ has a maximum value of 1, find the value of $a$.
   - **Options:** A. $1/2$, B. $1$, C. $3/2$, D. $2$
   - **Format:** Multiple choice, step-by-step reasoning, final answer in `\boxed{}`.

2.  **Understand the Mathematical Problem:**
   - Function: $f(x) = \frac{x+2}{e^x + a}$
   - Condition: The maximum value of $f(x)$ is 1.
   - Goal: Find the parameter $a$.
   - Domain: Implicitly $x \in \mathbb{R}$, and we need $e^x + a \neq 0$. Since $e^x > 0$, if $a \ge 0$, the denominator is always positive. Given the options, $a > 0$, so domain is $\mathbb{R}$.

3.  **Find the Maximum Value Condition:**
   - To find the maximum, take the derivative $f'(x)$ and set it to 0.
   - $f'(x) = \frac{(1)(e^x + a) - (x+2)(e^x)}{(e^x + a)^2} = \frac{e^x + a - xe^x - 2e^x}{(e^x + a)^2} = \frac{a - (x+1)e^x}{(e^x + a)^2}$
   - Set $f'(x) = 0 \Rightarrow a - (x+1)e^x = 0 \Rightarrow (x+1)e^x = a$.
   - Let $g(x) = (x+1)e^x$. We need to solve $g(x) = a$.
   - Analyze $g(x)$: $g'(x) = e^x + (x+1)e^x = (x+2)e^x$.
   - $g'(x) = 0 \Rightarrow x = -2$.
   - For $x < -2$, $g'(x) < 0$ (decreasing). For $x > -2$, $g'(x) > 0$ (increasing).
   - Minimum of $g(x)$ is at $x = -2$: $g(-2) = (-1)e^{-2} = -e^{-2}$.
   - Since $a > 0$ (from options), the equation $(x+1)e^x = a$ has exactly one solution for $x > -1$ (actually for $x > -1$, $g(x) > 0$, and $g(x) \to 0$ as $x \to -1$, $g(x) \to \infty$ as $x \to \infty$). Wait, let's check: $g(-1) = 0$. For $x > -1$, $g(x)$ increases from 0 to $\infty$. So for any $a > 0$, there is a unique critical point $x_0 > -1$ such that $(x_0+1)e^{x_0} = a$.
   - At this critical point $x_0$, $f(x)$ has a maximum (check second derivative or sign change of $f'$: $f'$ goes from + to - because $a - (x+1)e^x$ goes from positive to negative as $x$ increases through $x_0$). So $x_0$ is indeed a global maximum.

4.  **Use the Maximum Value Condition:**
   - We know $f(x_0) = 1$.
   - $f(x_0) = \frac{x_0+2}{e^{x_0} + a} = 1 \Rightarrow x_0 + 2 = e^{x_0} + a$.
   - We also have the critical point condition: $a = (x_0+1)e^{x_0}$.
   - Substitute $a$ into the first equation:
     $x_0 + 2 = e^{x_0} + (x_0+1)e^{x_0} = e^{x_0}(1 + x_0 + 1) = e^{x_0}(x_0 + 2)$.
   - So, $x_0 + 2 = (x_0 + 2)e^{x_0}$.
   - This implies either $x_0 + 2 = 0$ or $e^{x_0} = 1$.
   - Case 1: $x_0 + 2 = 0 \Rightarrow x_0 = -2$.
     But we found earlier that the critical point satisfies $(x_0+1)e^{x_0} = a$. If $x_0 = -2$, then $a = (-1)e^{-2} = -e^{-2} < 0$. But options are positive. Also, for $x_0 = -2$, $f(-2) = \frac{0}{e^{-2}+a} = 0 \neq 1$. So this case is invalid.
   - Case 2: $e^{x_0} = 1 \Rightarrow x_0 = 0$.
     Check if $x_0 = 0$ is valid.
     If $x_0 = 0$, then from $a = (x_0+1)e^{x_0}$, we get $a = (0+1)e^0 = 1$.
     Let's verify: If $a = 1$, then $f(x) = \frac{x+2}{e^x + 1}$.
     Find max: $f'(x) = \frac{1 - (x+1)e^x}{(e^x+1)^2}$. Set to 0: $(x+1)e^x = 1$. At $x=0$, $(0+1)e^0 = 1$. So $x=0$ is a critical point.
     $f(0) = \frac{0+2}{e^0+1} = \frac{2}{2} = 1$.
     Check if it's a maximum: For $x < 0$, $(x+1)e^x < 1$ (since $g(x)$ increases and $g(0)=1$), so $f'(x) > 0$. For $x > 0$, $(x+1)e^x > 1$, so $f'(x) < 0$. Thus $x=0$ is a global maximum.
     Maximum value is indeed 1.
     So $a = 1$ works.

5.  **Match with Options:**
   - $a = 1$ corresponds to option B.

6.  **Structure the Step-by-Step Reasoning:**
   - Step 1: Write down the function and find its derivative.
   - Step 2: Set derivative to zero to find critical point condition: $(x+1)e^x = a$.

   - Step 5: Get $x_0+2 = (x_0+2)e^{x_0}$, leading to $x_0=0$ (since $x_0 \neq -2$ for positive max).
   - Step 6: Calculate $a$ using $x_0=0$: $a = (0+1)e^0 = 1$.
   - Step 7: Verify the result matches option B.
   - Step 8: Conclude with $\boxed{B}$.

   - Could there be other solutions? $x_0+2=0$ gives $x_0=-2$, but then $f(-2)=0 \neq 1$, so rejected.
   - Is $a=1$ the only solution? Yes, derived uniquely.

Prompt
请解答以下数学题（单选题）。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

7. 一百零八塔位于宁夏回族自治区青铜峡市，以其独特的建筑格局和深厚的历史文化闻名遐迩。该塔群共有108座塔，依山势自上而下排成12行，将第i行中塔的座数记为a_i(i=1,2,...,12)，其中a_1=1, a_2=a_3=3, a_4=a_5=5，且a_6, a_7, ..., a_{12}是一个首项为7，公差为2的等差数列。将a_1, a_2, ..., a_{12}分为6组，每组2个数，使得每组的2个数之和可构成一个项数为6且公差为d(d>0)的等差数列，则d = （ ）

D. 8

Response
**逐步推理过程：**

1. **列出数列各项并计算总和**
   根据题意，12行塔的座数依次为：
   $a_1=1$
   $a_2=a_3=3$
   $a_4=a_5=5$
   $a_6, a_7, \dots, a_{12}$ 是首项为7、公差为2的等差数列，即 $7, 9, 11, 13, 15, 17, 19$。
   因此，这12个数构成的多重集为：$\{1, 3, 3, 5, 5, 7, 9, 11, 13, 15, 17, 19\}$。
   计算总和：$1+3\times2+5\times2+\frac{7\times(7+19)}{2} = 17 + 91 = 108$，与“一百零八塔”相符。

2. **建立分组和的等差数列方程**
   将12个数分为6组，每组2个数，设每组的和依次为 $S_1, S_2, \dots, S_6$，且构成公差为 $d(d>0)$ 的等差数列。不妨设最小和为 $b_1$。
   根据等差数列求和公式，这6个和的总和为：
   $\sum_{i=1}^6 S_i = \frac{6(b_1 + b_6)}{2} = 3[2b_1 + (6-1)d] = 6b_1 + 15d$
   这6个和的总和必须等于原12个数的总和108，故有：
   $6b_1 + 15d = 108 \Rightarrow 2b_1 + 5d = 36$  ......①

3. **结合选项与取值范围筛选 $d$**
   由①式可知，$b_1, d$ 均为正整数，且 $2b_1 = 36 - 5d > 0 \Rightarrow 5d < 36 \Rightarrow d \le 7$。由此直接排除 **D选项（$d=8$）**。
   又因为 $2b_1$ 和 $36$ 均为偶数，所以 $5d$ 必为偶数，即 $d$ 必须为偶数。可能取值为 $2, 4, 6$。
   此外，原数列中最小的两个数为 $1$ 和 $3$，其和为 $4$，故等差数列的首项必须满足 $b_1 \ge 4$。
   - 若 $d=6$（C选项）：代入①得 $2b_1 = 36 - 30 = 6 \Rightarrow b_1 = 3$。但 $3 < 4$，实际无法配对得到和为3的两数，**排除C**。
   - 若 $d=2$（A选项）：代入①得 $2b_1 = 36 - 10 = 26 \Rightarrow b_1 = 13$。此时和的数列为 $13, 15, 17, 19, 21, 23$。原数列中最大数为 $19$，要使其和落在该数列中，只能与 $\le 4$ 的数配对（$19+4=23$），但集合中仅有 $1$ 和 $3$ 满足 $\le 4$，而 $19+1=20$，$19+3=22$ 均不在目标数列中，故 $19$ 无法配对，**排除A**。
   - 若 $d=4$（B选项）：代入①得 $2b_1 = 36 - 20 = 16 \Rightarrow b_1 = 8$。此时和的数列为 $8, 12, 16, 20, 24, 28$。

4. **验证 $d=4$ 的配对可行性**
   尝试将集合 $\{1, 3, 3, 5, 5, 7, 9, 11, 13, 15, 17, 19\}$ 配成上述和：
   - $1 + 11 = 12$
   - $3 + 5 = 8$
   - $3 + 13 = 16$
   - $5 + 15 = 20$
   - $7 + 17 = 24$
   - $9 + 19 = 28$
   所有数恰好各用一次，且分组和为 $8, 12, 16, 20, 24, 28$，构成公差 $d=4$ 的等差数列，完全符合题意。

综上所述，公差 $d$ 的值为 4。

    - $a_2 = a_3 = 3$
    - $a_4 = a_5 = 5$
    - $a_6, a_7, ..., a_{12}$ form an arithmetic progression with first term 7 and common difference 2.
    - The sequence $a_1, a_2, ..., a_{12}$ is divided into 6 pairs. Each pair's sum forms an arithmetic progression of 6 terms with common difference $d > 0$.
    - Find $d$.
    - Options: A. 2, B. 4, C. 6, D. 8.

2.  **Extract and List the Sequence $a_i$:**
    - $a_1 = 1$
    - $a_2 = 3$
    - $a_3 = 3$
    - $a_4 = 5$
    - $a_5 = 5$
    - $a_6 = 7$
    - $a_7 = 9$
    - $a_8 = 11$
    - $a_9 = 13$
    - $a_{10} = 15$
    - $a_{11} = 17$
    - $a_{12} = 19$

    - Sum of first 5 terms: $1 + 3 + 3 + 5 + 5 = 17$.
    - Sum of AP from $a_6$ to $a_{12}$: 7 terms, first term 7, last term 19. Sum = $\frac{7}{2}(7 + 19) = \frac{7}{2} \times 26 = 7 \times 13 = 91$.
    - Total sum = $17 + 91 = 108$. Matches the problem statement ("一百零八塔" = 108 pagodas). Good.

    So the multiset of numbers is: $\{1, 3, 3, 5, 5, 7, 9, 11, 13, 15, 17, 19\}$.

3.  **Analyze the Pairing Condition:**
    - We need to partition these 12 numbers into 6 pairs.
    - Let the sums of the pairs be $S_1, S_2, ..., S_6$.
    - These sums form an arithmetic progression with common difference $d > 0$.
    - Let the AP be $b_1, b_2, ..., b_6$ with common difference $d$.
    - The sum of all $S_i$ is equal to the sum of all $a_i$, which is 108.
    - So, $\sum_{i=1}^6 S_i = 108$.
    - The sum of an AP with 6 terms is $\frac{6}{2}(2b_1 + (6-1)d) = 3(2b_1 + 5d) = 6b_1 + 15d$.
    - Therefore, $6b_1 + 15d = 108$.
    - Divide by 3: $2b_1 + 5d = 36$.
    - Since $b_1$ and $d$ are integers (sums of integers are integers), we can solve for possible integer pairs $(b_1, d)$ with $d > 0$.
    - $2b_1 = 36 - 5d \Rightarrow b_1 = \frac{36 - 5d}{2}$.
    - Since $b_1$ must be a positive integer (sums of positive integers), $36 - 5d$ must be a positive even number.
    - $36 - 5d > 0 \Rightarrow 5d < 36 \Rightarrow d \le 7$.
    - $36 - 5d$ is even $\Rightarrow 5d$ is even $\Rightarrow d$ is even.
    - So possible even $d \in \{2, 4, 6\}$. (Matches options A, B, C. D is 8, which gives $36 - 40 = -4$, negative $b_1$, impossible).

Prompt
请解答以下数学题（单选题）。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

8. 设U={(x_1, x_2, x_3)|x_i \in \{-2,-1,1,2"}, i=1,2,3}\}为空间中64个点构成的集合，点P(1,1,1)，记样本空间\Omega = C_U P，从\Omega中随机取一个点，定义随机变量X如下：对\Omega中的每个点A(x_1, x_2, x_3)，令X(A) = x_1 + x_2 + x_3，则X的数学期望值为（ ）
A. -\frac{1}{21}
B. -\frac{1}{63}
C. 0
D. \frac{1}{7}

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
这是一道关于数学期望的单选题，我们通过以下步骤逐步推理：

### 1. 分析集合 $U$ 与随机变量 $X$ 的性质
*   **集合 $U$ 的大小**：集合 $U$ 中的每个坐标 $x_i$ 均取自 $\{-2, -1, 1, 2\}$。由于 $x_1, x_2, x_3$ 相互独立，集合 $U$ 包含的总点数是 $4 \times 4 \times 4 = 64$ 个。
*   **集合 $U$ 中坐标值的对称性**：观察坐标取值集合 $\{-2, -1, 1, 2\}$，其元素之和为 $-2 - 1 + 1 + 2 = 0$。
*   **计算 $U$ 中所有点 $X$ 值的总和**：
    对于任意一个坐标分量 $x_i$，在 64 个点中，每个取值（-2, -1, 1, 2）均出现了 $4 \times 4 = 16$ 次。
    因此，所有点的 $x_1$ 之和为 $16 \times (-2 - 1 + 1 + 2) = 0$。同理，$x_2$ 之和与 $x_3$ 之和也为 0。
    所以，集合 $U$ 中所有点的 $X(A) = x_1 + x_2 + x_3$ 的总和为 0。

### 2. 分析样本空间 $\Omega$
*   **样本空间的构成**：题目指出 $\Omega = C_U P$，即 $\Omega$ 是集合 $U$ 除去点 $P(1, 1, 1)$ 后的补集。
*   **样本空间的大小**：$\Omega$ 中的点数 $|\Omega| = 64 - 1 = 63$。
*   **随机取点的性质**：从 $\Omega$ 中随机取一个点，意味着每个点被取到的概率为 $\frac{1}{63}$。

### 3. 计算 $X$ 的数学期望 $E[X]$
*   **计算 $\Omega$ 中 $X$ 值的总和**：
    $\Omega$ 中所有点的 $X$ 值总和 = $U$ 中总和 - $P$ 点的 $X$ 值。
    $P(1, 1, 1)$ 的 $X$ 值为 $1 + 1 + 1 = 3$。
    因此，$\sum_{A \in \Omega} X(A) = 0 - 3 = -3$。
*   **计算期望值**：
    根据期望的定义：
    $$E[X] = \frac{\text{总和}}{\text{总点数}} = \frac{-3}{63}$$
    化简分数得：
    $$E[X] = -\frac{1}{21}$$

### 4. 结论
通过上述计算，$X$ 的数学期望值为 $-\frac{1}{21}$，对应选项 A。

\boxed{A}
Reasoning
Here's a thinking process to solve the problem:

1.  **Understand the Goal:** The objective is to calculate the expected value of a random variable $X$ defined on a specific sample space $\Omega$.

2.  **Analyze the Set $U$:**
    *   $U = \{(x_1, x_2, x_3) \mid x_i \in \{-2, -1, 1, 2\}, i=1,2,3\}$.
    *   Each coordinate $x_i$ can take 4 values.
    *   Total number of points in $U$ is $4 \times 4 \times 4 = 64$.

3.  **Analyze the Sample Space $\Omega$:**
    *   $\Omega = C_U P$ usually denotes the complement of $P$ in $U$ (often written as $U \setminus \{P\}$).
    *   $P = (1, 1, 1)$.
    *   So, $\Omega$ contains all points in $U$ except $(1, 1, 1)$.
    *   The size of $\Omega$ (number of elements) is $64 - 1 = 63$.
    *   The problem states "from $\Omega$ randomly take a point", implying a uniform distribution. Each point in $\Omega$ has a probability of $1/63$ of being selected.

4.  **Analyze the Random Variable $X$:**
    *   For a point $A(x_1, x_2, x_3) \in \Omega$, $X(A) = x_1 + x_2 + x_3$.
    *   We need to find $E[X]$.

5.  **Calculate the Expected Value $E[X]$:**
    *   Definition: $E[X] = \sum_{A \in \Omega} X(A) \cdot P(A)$.
    *   Since $P(A) = \frac{1}{63}$ for all $A \in \Omega$,
        $$E[X] = \frac{1}{63} \sum_{A \in \Omega} (x_1 + x_2 + x_3)$$
    *   Let $S_{total}$ be the sum of coordinates for *all* points in $U$.
    *   Let $S_{P}$ be the sum of coordinates for the point $P(1,1,1)$.
    *   Then $\sum_{A \in \Omega} X(A) = S_{total} - S_{P}$.
    *   $S_{P} = 1 + 1 + 1 = 3$.

6.  **Calculate $S_{total}$ (Sum over $U$):**
    *   $U$ is the set of all triples $(x_1, x_2, x_3)$ where $x_i \in \{-2, -1, 1, 2\}$.
    *   $S_{total} = \sum_{(x_1, x_2, x_3) \in U} (x_1 + x_2 + x_3)$.
    *   This sum can be split into three parts:
        $$S_{total} = \sum_{(x_1, x_2, x_3) \in U} x_1 + \sum_{(x_1, x_2, x_3) \in U} x_2 + \sum_{(x_1, x_2, x_3) \in U} x_3$$
    *   By symmetry, these three sums are equal. Let's calculate the first one:
        $$\sum_{(x_1, x_2, x_3) \in U} x_1$$
    *   Here, $x_2$ and $x_3$ vary independently. For a fixed $x_1$, there are 4 choices for $x_2$ and 4 choices for $x_3$, so 16 pairs $(x_2, x_3)$.
    *   The possible values for $x_1$ are $\{-2, -1, 1, 2\}$.
    *   Sum = $\sum_{x_1 \in \{-2, -1, 1, 2\}} x_1 \times (\text{number of pairs } (x_2, x_3))$
    *   Number of pairs = $4 \times 4 = 16$.
    *   Sum of $x_1$ values = $(-2) + (-1) + 1 + 2 = 0$.
    *   Therefore, $\sum x_1 = 16 \times 0 = 0$.
    *   Similarly, $\sum x_2 = 0$ and $\sum x_3 = 0$.
    *   So, $S_{total} = 0 + 0 + 0 = 0$.

    *Alternative way to see this:* The distribution of each coordinate $x_i$ is uniform over $\{-2, -1, 1, 2\}$.
    $E[x_i] = \frac{1}{4}(-2 -1 + 1 + 2) = 0$.
    $E[\text{Sum}] = E[x_1] + E[x_2] + E[x_3] = 0 + 0 + 0 = 0$.
    Total sum = $E[\text{Sum}] \times (\text{Total number of points}) = 0 \times 64 = 0$.

7.  **Calculate Sum over $\Omega$:**
    *   $\sum_{A \in \Omega} X(A) = S_{total} - S_{P} = 0 - 3 = -3$.

8.  **Calculate $E[X]$:**
    *   $E[X] = \frac{1}{|\Omega|} \times (\text{Sum over } \Omega)$
    *   $|\Omega| = 63$.
    *   $E[X] = \frac{-3}{63}$.
    *   Simplify fraction: $\frac{-3}{63} = -\frac{1}{21}$.

9.  **Check Options:**
    A. $-\frac{1}{21}$
    B. $-\frac{1}{63}$
    C. $0$
    D. $\frac{1}{7}$

    The calculated result matches Option A.

10. **Double Check:**
    *   **Set $U$**: $x_i \in \{-2, -1, 1, 2\}$. Note: 0 is missing. This is important. If 0 were included, symmetry would still hold. But wait, sum is $-2-1+1+2=0$. Yes.
    *   **Point $P$**: $(1,1,1)$. Sum is 3.
    *   **Set $\Omega$**: $U \setminus \{P\}$. Size 63.

        Number of points = 64.
        Average value of $x_1$: $(-2-1+1+2)/4 = 0$.

        Total sum over $U$: $64 \times 0 = 0$.
    *   **Sum over $\Omega$**: $0 - 3 = -3$.
    *   **Expectation**: $-3 / 63 = -1/21$.

gaokao_000_008 ✓ ACD ACD 2253 55.5 40.6 http://192.168.0.41:9292
Prompt
请解答以下数学题（多选题）。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

9. 设z = 3 + 2i，则（ ）
A. \bar{z} = 3 - 2i
B. |z| = 5
C. z^2 = 5 + 12i
D. \frac{z+3}{z-i} \in \mathbf{R}

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
已知复数 $z = 3 + 2i$，逐项分析如下：

**A. $\bar{z} = 3 - 2i$**
复数的共轭复数定义为实部不变，虚部变号。对于 $z = a + bi$，有 $\bar{z} = a - bi$。
此处 $a=3, b=2$，故 $\bar{z} = 3 - 2i$。
✅ **选项A正确。**

**B. $|z| = 5$**
复数的模定义为 $|z| = \sqrt{a^2 + b^2}$。
计算得 $|z| = \sqrt{3^2 + 2^2} = \sqrt{9 + 4} = \sqrt{13} \neq 5$。
❌ **选项B错误。**

**C. $z^2 = 5 + 12i$**
直接展开计算：
$z^2 = (3 + 2i)^2 = 3^2 + 2 \cdot 3 \cdot 2i + (2i)^2 = 9 + 12i + 4i^2$
因为 $i^2 = -1$，所以 $z^2 = 9 + 12i - 4 = 5 + 12i$。
✅ **选项C正确。**

**D. $\frac{z+3}{z-i} \in \mathbf{R}$**
将 $z = 3 + 2i$ 代入表达式：
分子：$z + 3 = (3 + 2i) + 3 = 6 + 2i$
分母：$z - i = (3 + 2i) - i = 3 + i$
则 $\frac{z+3}{z-i} = \frac{6+2i}{3+i} = \frac{2(3+i)}{3+i} = 2$
因为 $2$ 是实数，即 $2 \in \mathbf{R}$，所以该复数为实数。
✅ **选项D正确。**

综上所述，正确选项为 A、C、D。

\boxed{ACD}
Reasoning
Here's a thinking process:

1.  **Analyze User Input:**
   - **Question Type:** Multiple-choice question (多选题 - multiple correct answers possible)
   - **Given:** Complex number $z = 3 + 2i$
   - **Options:**
     - A. $\bar{z} = 3 - 2i$
     - B. $|z| = 5$
     - C. $z^2 = 5 + 12i$
     - D. $\frac{z+3}{z-i} \in \mathbf{R}$ (The result is a real number)
   - **Task:** Step-by-step reasoning, final answer in `\boxed{}`.

2.  **Evaluate Each Option:**

   - **Option A:** $\bar{z} = 3 - 2i$
     - Definition of complex conjugate: If $z = a + bi$, then $\bar{z} = a - bi$.
     - Here, $a = 3$, $b = 2$, so $\bar{z} = 3 - 2i$.
     - Conclusion: **A is correct.**

   - **Option B:** $|z| = 5$
     - Definition of modulus: $|z| = \sqrt{a^2 + b^2}$.
     - Here, $|z| = \sqrt{3^2 + 2^2} = \sqrt{9 + 4} = \sqrt{13}$.
     - $\sqrt{13} \neq 5$.
     - Conclusion: **B is incorrect.**

   - **Option C:** $z^2 = 5 + 12i$
     - Calculate $z^2 = (3 + 2i)^2$.
     - $(3 + 2i)^2 = 3^2 + 2 \cdot 3 \cdot 2i + (2i)^2 = 9 + 12i + 4i^2$.
     - Since $i^2 = -1$, this becomes $9 + 12i - 4 = 5 + 12i$.
     - Conclusion: **C is correct.**

   - **Option D:** $\frac{z+3}{z-i} \in \mathbf{R}$
     - Substitute $z = 3 + 2i$ into the expression:
       Numerator: $z + 3 = (3 + 2i) + 3 = 6 + 2i$.
       Denominator: $z - i = (3 + 2i) - i = 3 + i$.
     - Expression: $\frac{6 + 2i}{3 + i}$.
     - Simplify by multiplying numerator and denominator by the conjugate of the denominator ($3 - i$):
       $\frac{(6 + 2i)(3 - i)}{(3 + i)(3 - i)} = \frac{18 - 6i + 6i - 2i^2}{3^2 + 1^2} = \frac{18 - 2(-1)}{9 + 1} = \frac{18 + 2}{10} = \frac{20}{10} = 2$.
     - The result is $2$, which is a real number ($2 \in \mathbf{R}$).
     - Conclusion: **D is correct.**

3.  **Synthesize Results:**
   - Correct options: A, C, D.
   - Incorrect option: B.

     - C: $(3+2i)^2 = 9 + 12i - 4 = 5 + 12i$. Correct.
     - D: $\frac{6+2i}{3+i} = \frac{2(3+i)}{3+i} = 2 \in \mathbb{R}$. (Even faster way: notice numerator is exactly 2 times denominator). Correct.
   - All checks pass.

gaokao_000_009 ✓ BC BC 21326 49.1 434.3 http://192.168.0.41:9292
Prompt
请解答以下数学题（多选题）。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

10. 在空间中，A、B为两个定点，动点C到直线AB的距离为2，动点D到直线AB的距离为1。若二面角C - AB - D为60°，则（ ）
A. \angle CAD \ge 60^\circ
B. CD \ge \sqrt{3}
C. 当AB \perp CD时，CD \perp平面ABD
D. 当AB \perp平面ACD时，AC \perp AD

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
这是一道关于立体几何中动点轨迹、距离及垂直关系的多选题。我们可以通过建立空间直角坐标系来逐步分析每个选项。

### 1. 建立坐标系与设定坐标
设定点 $A$ 为原点 $(0, 0, 0)$，直线 $AB$ 为 $z$ 轴。
*   根据动点 $D$ 到 $z$ 轴距离为 $1$，设 $D$ 的坐标为 $(1, 0, z_D)$。
*   根据动点 $C$ 到 $z$ 轴距离为 $2$ 且二面角 $C-AB-D$ 为 $60^\circ$，设 $C$ 的坐标为 $(2\cos60^\circ, 2\sin60^\circ, z_C) = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_C)$。

### 2. 逐项分析

**A. $\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$**
向量 $\vec{AC} = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_C)$，$\vec{AD} = (1, 0, z_D)$。
$\cos \angle CAD = \frac{1 + z_C z_D}{\sqrt{4+z_C^2}\sqrt{1+z_D^2}}$。
当 $z_C = z_D = 10$ 时，$\cos \angle CAD \approx \frac{101}{\sqrt{104}\sqrt{101}} \approx 0.98 > 0.5$。
此时 $\angle CAD$ 接近 $0^\circ$，故 A 错误。

**B. $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$**
距离平方 $CD^2 = (1-1)^2 + (0-\sqrt{3})^2 + (z_D - z_C)^2 = 3 + (z_D - z_C)^2$。
由于 $(z_D - z_C)^2 \ge 0$，则 $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$。
当 $z_C = z_D$ 时取到最小值 $\sqrt{3}$，故 B 正确。

**C. 当 $AB \perp CD$ 时，$CD \perp$ 平面 $ABD$**
条件 $AB \perp CD$ 等价于向量 $(0,0,1) \cdot (0, -\sqrt{3}, z_D-z_C) = 0$，即 $z_C = z_D$。
此时 $\vec{CD} = (0, -\sqrt{3}, 0)$，该向量垂直于 $z$ 轴。
平面 $ABD$ 包含 $z$ 轴和点 $D(1, 0, z_C)$，该平面即为 $y=0$ 平面，其法向量为 $(0, 1, 0)$。
由于 $\vec{CD}$ 与平面 $ABD$ 的法向量平行，故 $CD \perp$ 平面 $ABD$。C 正确。

**D. 当 $AB \perp$ 平面 $ACD$ 时，$AC \perp AD$**
条件 $AB \perp$ 平面 $ACD$ 意味着 $AB \perp AC$ 且 $AB \perp AD$，即 $z_C = 0$ 且 $z_D = 0$。
此时 $\vec{AC} = (1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$，$\vec{AD} = (1, 0, 0)$。
$\vec{AC} \cdot \vec{AD} = 1 \ne 0$，故 $AC$ 与 $AD$ 不垂直。D 错误。

### 结论
正确的选项是 B 和 C。

\boxed{BC}
Reasoning
Here's a thinking process to solve the problem:

1.  **Understand the Goal:** The problem asks to analyze the geometric relationship between points A, B, C, D in space given specific constraints:
    *   A and B are fixed points (defining a line $l = AB$).
    *   Point C moves such that its distance to line $AB$ is 2.
    *   Point D moves such that its distance to line $AB$ is 1.
    *   The dihedral angle $C-AB-D$ is $60^\circ$.
    *   Need to evaluate four options (A, B, C, D) to see which are correct.

2.  **Setup a Coordinate System:**
    *   Let line $AB$ be the $z$-axis (or $x$-axis, but $z$ is standard for vertical height). Let's use the $x$-axis for line $AB$ to align with standard plane geometry visualization first, or just pick a convenient one.
    *   Let line $AB$ coincide with the $z$-axis.
    *   Point A is at the origin $(0,0,0)$? Wait, A and B are fixed, but their distance isn't specified. However, the distance of C and D to the *line* AB depends only on the perpendicular distance. The relative position of A and B on the line matters for angles like $\angle CAD$, so let's fix coordinates.
    *   Let $A = (0, 0, 0)$.
    *   Let $B = (0, 0, b)$ for some $b \neq 0$. Actually, the direction of vector $\vec{AB}$ is just the direction of the axis.
    *   Let's set the line $AB$ as the $x$-axis.
        *   $A = (0, 0, 0)$.
        *   $B = (L, 0, 0)$ where $L > 0$.
    *   Distance of a point $P(x,y,z)$ to the $x$-axis is $\sqrt{y^2 + z^2}$.
    *   Condition 1: Distance of $C$ to $x$-axis is 2. So $C = (x_C, y_C, z_C)$ where $y_C^2 + z_C^2 = 2^2 = 4$.
    *   Condition 2: Distance of $D$ to $x$-axis is 1. So $D = (x_D, y_D, z_D)$ where $y_D^2 + z_D^2 = 1^2 = 1$.
    *   Condition 3: Dihedral angle $C-AB-D$ is $60^\circ$.
        *   The "edges" of the dihedral angle are planes passing through the line $AB$.
        *   The plane containing $C$ and line $AB$ makes an angle with the plane containing $D$ and line $AB$.
        *   The normal vectors to these planes are perpendicular to the $x$-axis.
        *   Let's project $C$ and $D$ onto the $yz$-plane (perpendicular to $AB$).
        *   Let $C'$ be the projection of $C$ on the $yz$-plane. $C' = (0, y_C, z_C)$. Since distance is 2, $C'$ is on a circle of radius 2.
        *   Let $D'$ be the projection of $D$ on the $yz$-plane. $D' = (0, y_D, z_D)$. Since distance is 1, $D'$ is on a circle of radius 1.
        *   The angle between the vectors $\vec{OC'}$ and $\vec{OD'}$ (where O is origin) represents the dihedral angle?
        *   Wait, the dihedral angle is the angle between the half-planes defined by the line AB.
        *   Yes, if we look down the $x$-axis, the points $C'$ and $D'$ are on circles. The angle between the radius vectors to $C'$ and $D'$ is the dihedral angle.
        *   Let's define the angles in the $yz$-plane.
        *   Let $C'$ be at angle $\alpha$. Coordinates in $yz$ plane: $(2\cos\alpha, 2\sin\alpha)$.
        *   Let $D'$ be at angle $\beta$. Coordinates in $yz$ plane: $(1\cos\beta, 1\sin\beta)$.
        *   The dihedral angle is $|\alpha - \beta| = 60^\circ$ (or $\pi/3$).
        *   Without loss of generality, we can rotate the coordinate system around the $x$-axis. Let's set $D$'s projection on the positive $y$-axis?
        *   Let's place $D$ in the $xy$-plane? No, $AB$ is $x$-axis.
        *   Let's simplify.
        *   Line $AB$ is the $x$-axis.
        *   Plane $ABD$ can be taken as the $xy$-plane? No, if $D$ is in $xy$-plane, its distance to $x$-axis is just $|y_D|$.
        *   Let's fix the position of $D$ to simplify.
        *   Let $D = (x_D, 1, 0)$. Then distance to $x$-axis is 1.
        *   Since the dihedral angle is $60^\circ$, point $C$ must be in a plane that makes a $60^\circ$ angle with the $xy$-plane.
        *   So, in the $yz$-plane, the angle of $C$'s projection is $60^\circ$ relative to $D$'s projection.
        *   Let's define coordinates:
            *   $A = (0, 0, 0)$.
            *   $B = (b, 0, 0)$.
            *   $D = (x_D, 1, 0)$ is not quite right because the distance is $\sqrt{y^2+z^2}=1$.
            *   Actually, distance is the perpendicular distance.
            *   Let $C = (u, 2\cos 60^\circ, 2\sin 60^\circ)$? No, that fixes the angle.
            *   Let's formalize.
            *   Let line $AB$ be the $z$-axis? Maybe easier for "distance to axis".
            *   Let $A = (0, 0, 0)$.
            *   Let line $AB$ be the $z$-axis.
            *   Distance of $C$ to $z$-axis is $\sqrt{x_C^2 + y_C^2} = 2$.
            *   Distance of $D$ to $z$-axis is $\sqrt{x_D^2 + y_D^2} = 1$.
            *   The dihedral angle is the angle between the plane containing $z$-axis and $C$, and plane containing $z$-axis and $D$.
            *   This is the difference in polar angles in the $xy$-plane.
            *   Let $C$'s polar angle be $\theta_C$ and $D$'s polar angle be $\theta_D$.
            *   $|\theta_C - \theta_D| = 60^\circ$.
            *   We can fix $D$ on the $x$-axis? No, $D$ is a point in space.
            *   Let's fix $D$ to be $(1, 0, z_D)$. Wait, distance to $z$-axis is 1. So $x^2+y^2=1$. Let's choose $D$ on the $x$-axis in projection? Yes.
            *   Let $D = (1, 0, d)$ where $d$ is the $z$-coordinate.
            *   Then $C$ must have a projection angle of $60^\circ$ (or $-60^\circ$).
            *   $C = (2\cos 60^\circ, 2\sin 60^\circ, c) = (2 \cdot \frac{1}{2}, 2 \cdot \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, c) = (1, \sqrt{3}, c)$.
            *   Here $c, d$ are arbitrary real numbers (positions along the line $AB$).
            *   Wait, A and B are fixed. $A$ is $(0,0,0)$. $B$ is $(0,0,b)$.
            *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, c)$.
            *   $D = (1, 0, d)$.
            *   Note: The distance from $C$ to $z$-axis is $\sqrt{1^2 + (\sqrt{3})^2} = 2$. Correct.
            *   The distance from $D$ to $z$-axis is $\sqrt{1^2 + 0^2} = 1$. Correct.
            *   The angle between vector $(1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$ and $(1, 0, 0)$ is $60^\circ$. Correct.
            *   So the coordinates are:
                *   $A = (0, 0, 0)$
                *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, c)$
                *   $D = (1, 0, d)$
                *   (Note: The choice of angle sign doesn't affect distances or angles between points, just orientation).
            *   Actually, I can shift coordinates so $A$ is origin, which is done.
            *   What about $B$? $B$ is some point on $z$-axis. $B=(0,0,z_B)$.
            *   However, looking at the options, they involve $AC$, $AD$, $CD$, $AB$.
            *   $A$ is the origin. $C$ and $D$ depend on parameters $c, d$ (their height along the axis).
            *   $B$ is just defining the line. If an option depends on $B$, I need to be careful.
            *   Let's check the options.

3.  **Analyze Option A: $\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$**
    *   Vector $\vec{AC} = (1, \sqrt{3}, c)$.
    *   Vector $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, d)$.
    *   Dot product $\vec{AC} \cdot \vec{AD} = 1\cdot1 + \sqrt{3}\cdot0 + c\cdot d = 1 + cd$.
    *   Lengths: $|\vec{AC}| = \sqrt{1^2 + (\sqrt{3})^2 + c^2} = \sqrt{4 + c^2}$.
    *   $|\vec{AD}| = \sqrt{1^2 + 0^2 + d^2} = \sqrt{1 + d^2}$.
    *   $\cos(\angle CAD) = \frac{\vec{AC} \cdot \vec{AD}}{|\vec{AC}| |\vec{AD}|} = \frac{1 + cd}{\sqrt{4 + c^2} \sqrt{1 + d^2}}$.
    *   We need to check if $\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$, which means $\cos(\angle CAD) \le \cos(60^\circ) = 1/2$.
    *   So, is $\frac{1 + cd}{\sqrt{4 + c^2} \sqrt{1 + d^2}} \le \frac{1}{2}$ always true?
    *   Let's test specific values.
    *   Let $c = 0, d = 0$. (Points $C, D$ in $xy$-plane).
        *   $\cos \theta = \frac{1}{\sqrt{4}\sqrt{1}} = \frac{1}{2}$.
        *   $\theta = 60^\circ$. So equality holds.
    *   Let $c$ be very large positive, $d$ be very large positive.
        *   $c=d=k \gg 1$.
        *   $\cos \theta \approx \frac{k^2}{\sqrt{k^2}\sqrt{k^2}} = 1$.
        *   Angle $\to 0^\circ$.
        *   Wait, if angle is $0^\circ$, then $\angle CAD < 60^\circ$.
    *   Let's verify this intuition.
        *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, k)$. $D = (1, 0, k)$.
        *   $A = (0, 0, 0)$.
        *   $C$ is far up, $D$ is far up. They are close to each other relative to distance from origin.
        *   $C$ projects to $(1, \sqrt{3})$ on $xy$, $D$ projects to $(1,0)$.
        *   Distance $CD = \sqrt{(1-1)^2 + (\sqrt{3}-0)^2 + (k-k)^2} = \sqrt{3}$.
        *   $AC = \sqrt{4 + k^2}$.
        *   $AD = \sqrt{1 + k^2}$.
        *   By Law of Cosines on $\triangle ACD$:
            $CD^2 = AC^2 + AD^2 - 2 AC \cdot AD \cos \theta$
            $3 = (4+k^2) + (1+k^2) - 2\sqrt{4+k^2}\sqrt{1+k^2} \cos \theta$
            $3 = 5 + 2k^2 - 2\sqrt{(4+k^2)(1+k^2)} \cos \theta$
            $2\sqrt{(4+k^2)(1+k^2)} \cos \theta = 2 + 2k^2$
            $\cos \theta = \frac{2+2k^2}{2\sqrt{4+k^2}\sqrt{1+k^2}} = \frac{1+k^2}{\sqrt{4+k^2}\sqrt{1+k^2}}$.
        *   As $k \to \infty$, $\cos \theta \to 1$, so $\theta \to 0$.
    *   So $\angle CAD$ can be smaller than $60^\circ$.
    *   Therefore, **Option A is False**.
    *   *Self-Correction Check*: Did I interpret "distance to line AB" correctly? Yes. "Dihedral angle" correctly? Yes.
    *   Wait, is $A$ fixed? Yes. But $C$ and $D$ can move *along* the direction parallel to $AB$.
    *   The problem says "A, B are fixed points". "Moving point C...". C can be anywhere as long as distance to line AB is 2. It doesn't say C is in a plane perpendicular to AB. C is in space.
    *   So my coordinate setup $C=(1, \sqrt{3}, c)$ covers all valid positions of C (rotated appropriately).
    *   So A is definitely false.

4.  **Analyze Option B: $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$**
    *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, c)$.
    *   $D = (1, 0, d)$.
    *   Distance $CD = \sqrt{(1-1)^2 + (\sqrt{3}-0)^2 + (c-d)^2} = \sqrt{3 + (c-d)^2}$.
    *   Since $(c-d)^2 \ge 0$, $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$.
    *   Equality holds when $c=d$, i.e., $C$ and $D$ have the same $z$-coordinate (same position along line AB).
    *   Is there any constraint preventing $c=d$?
    *   C distance to AB is 2. D distance to AB is 1.
    *   Dihedral angle 60.
    *   These constraints are satisfied by $C=(1, \sqrt{3}, c)$ and $D=(1, 0, c)$.
    *   Distance is $\sqrt{3}$.
    *   Can it be smaller? No, $(c-d)^2$ is non-negative.
    *   Wait, let's re-read carefully.
    *   Distance between two lines in space? No, points C and D.
    *   Geometric interpretation:
        *   Project C and D onto a plane perpendicular to AB. Let these be $C'$ and $D'$.
        *   $O$ is the intersection of the plane and line AB.
        *   $OC' = 2$, $OD' = 1$.
        *   $\angle C'OD' = 60^\circ$.
        *   By Law of Cosines in $\triangle OC'D'$, $C'D'^2 = 2^2 + 1^2 - 2(2)(1)\cos(60^\circ) = 4 + 1 - 2 = 3$.
        *   So $C'D' = \sqrt{3}$.
        *   The distance between points C and D in 3D space is given by $CD^2 = C'D'^2 + (z_C - z_D)^2$, where $z$ is the coordinate along AB.
        *   So $CD^2 = 3 + (z_C - z_D)^2 \ge 3$.
        *   $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$.
    *   So **Option B is True**.

5.  **Analyze Option C: When $AB \perp CD$, $CD \perp$ plane $ABD$**
    *   Condition: $AB \perp CD$.
    *   Vector $\vec{AB}$ is along the $z$-axis (direction $(0,0,1)$).
    *   Vector $\vec{CD} = D - C = (1-1, 0-\sqrt{3}, d-c) = (0, -\sqrt{3}, d-c)$.
    *   Dot product $\vec{AB} \cdot \vec{CD} = 0 \cdot 0 + 0 \cdot (-\sqrt{3}) + 1 \cdot (d-c) = d-c$.
    *   For $AB \perp CD$, we need $d-c = 0$, so $d=c$.
    *   So C and D are at the same "height" along the line AB.
    *   If $d=c$, then $\vec{CD} = (0, -\sqrt{3}, 0)$. This vector is purely in the $y$-direction (perpendicular to $z$-axis).
    *   We need to check if $CD \perp$ plane $ABD$.
    *   Plane $ABD$ contains line $AB$ (the $z$-axis) and point $D(1, 0, c)$.
    *   Since $D$ is on the $x$-axis (shifted by $z=c$), the plane $ABD$ is the $xz$-plane (plane $y=0$)?
        *   Wait, coordinates were: $A=(0,0,0)$, $B$ on $z$-axis, $D=(1,0,c)$.
        *   Yes, $A, B, D$ all have $y=0$.
        *   So plane $ABD$ is the plane $y=0$ (the $xz$-plane).
    *   Vector $\vec{CD} = (0, -\sqrt{3}, 0)$.
    *   Is $\vec{CD}$ perpendicular to the plane $y=0$?
    *   The normal to $y=0$ is $(0,1,0)$.
    *   $\vec{CD}$ is parallel to $(0,1,0)$ (specifically $-\sqrt{3}(0,1,0)$).
    *   So yes, $CD$ is perpendicular to plane $ABD$.
    *   Let's double check this geometric intuition.
    *   $AB$ is a line.
    *   $C$ and $D$ are points.
    *   $CD \perp AB$ means the segment $CD$ is perpendicular to the line $AB$.
    *   Since $C$ and $D$ project to $C', D'$ in a plane $\perp AB$, $CD \perp AB$ implies $C$ and $D$ have the same projection onto $AB$, i.e., they lie on the same plane perpendicular to $AB$.
    *   So $C$ and $D$ are in a plane $\perp AB$.
    *   In this plane, $O$ (intersection with AB) is the origin. $OC=2, OD=1, \angle COD = 60^\circ$.
    *   Vector $\vec{CD} = \vec{OD} - \vec{OC}$.
    *   Plane $ABD$ contains line $AB$ and point $D$.
    *   Since $AB \perp$ the plane containing $C, D$ (let's call it $\pi$), line $AB$ is perpendicular to every line in $\pi$ passing through $O$? No, $AB$ is normal to $\pi$.
    *   So plane $ABD$ passes through the normal of $\pi$ (which is AB) and point $D$.
    *   Thus plane $ABD$ is perpendicular to $\pi$.
    *   The intersection of plane $ABD$ and $\pi$ is the line $OD$.
    *   The line $CD$ lies in $\pi$.
    *   For $CD$ to be perpendicular to plane $ABD$, $CD$ must be perpendicular to every line in $ABD$. Specifically $CD \perp AB$ (given) and $CD \perp OD$? No, $CD$ connects C and D.
    *   Wait, if a line ($CD$) is perpendicular to a plane ($ABD$), it must be perpendicular to two intersecting lines in that plane.
    *   We know $CD \perp AB$.
    *   Is $CD \perp AD$? Or $CD \perp BD$?
    *   Since $C, D$ are in plane $\pi$ perpendicular to $AB$, and $A$ is on $AB$, the projection of $A$ onto $\pi$ is $O$ (assuming A is origin? No, A is on the line).
    *   Let's check the projection. $A$ is on line $AB$. Plane $\pi$ cuts $AB$ at some point $P$.
    *   If $C, D$ are in $\pi$, then $AC$ and $AD$ are hypotenuses of right triangles $APC$ and $APD$?
    *   Wait, if $AB \perp CD$, then $C$ and $D$ are at the same distance from $A$ along the axis? No.
    *   Let's stick to the coordinates. It's safer.
    *   $A = (0,0,0)$. $B$ is on $z$-axis.
    *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, c)$. $D = (1, 0, d)$.
    *   $CD \perp AB \implies d = c$.
    *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, c)$, $D = (1, 0, c)$.
    *   Vector $\vec{CD} = (0, -\sqrt{3}, 0)$.
    *   Plane $ABD$ passes through $A(0,0,0)$, $B(0,0,b)$, $D(1,0,c)$.
    *   Since $A, B$ are on $z$-axis, and $D$ has $y=0$, the plane containing them is $y=0$.
    *   Normal vector to plane $ABD$ is $\vec{n} = (0, 1, 0)$.
    *   Vector $\vec{CD} = (0, -\sqrt{3}, 0) = -\sqrt{3} (0, 1, 0)$.
    *   So $\vec{CD}$ is parallel to the normal vector.
    *   Therefore, $CD \perp$ plane $ABD$.
    *   **Option C is True**.

    *   *Self-Correction / Verification*: Is it possible that $A, B, D$ are collinear?
    *   $A=(0,0,0)$, $B=(0,0,b)$, $D=(1,0,c)$.
    *   $D$ is not on the $z$-axis (distance to AB is 1). So $A, B, D$ form a plane.
    *   Is it possible that $C, D$ coincide?
    *   Distance to AB is 2 and 1. Cannot coincide.
    *   So the logic holds.

6.  **Analyze Option D: When $AB \perp$ plane $ACD$, $AC \perp AD$**
    *   Condition: $AB \perp$ plane $ACD$.
    *   This means line $AB$ is perpendicular to every line in plane $ACD$.
    *   Specifically, $AB \perp AC$ and $AB \perp AD$.
    *   Let's check coordinates.
    *   $\vec{AB}$ is along $z$-axis (0,0,1).
    *   $A = (0,0,0)$.
    *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, c)$.
    *   $D = (1, 0, d)$.
    *   $\vec{AC} = (1, \sqrt{3}, c)$.
    *   $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, d)$.
    *   $AB \perp AC \implies \vec{AB} \cdot \vec{AC} = 0 \implies c = 0$.
    *   $AB \perp AD \implies \vec{AB} \cdot \vec{AD} = 0 \implies d = 0$.
    *   So, if $AB \perp$ plane $ACD$, then $c=0$ and $d=0$.
    *   This places $C$ and $D$ in the $xy$-plane (perpendicular to $AB$ at $A$).
    *   So $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$ and $D = (1, 0, 0)$.
    *   We need to check if $AC \perp AD$.
    *   $\vec{AC} = (1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$.
    *   $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, 0)$.
    *   Dot product $\vec{AC} \cdot \vec{AD} = 1\cdot1 + \sqrt{3}\cdot0 + 0 = 1$.
    *   For perpendicularity, dot product must be 0.
    *   Here it is 1.
    *   So $AC$ is not perpendicular to $AD$.
    *   The angle is $\cos \theta = \frac{1}{2\cdot 1} = 1/2 \implies 60^\circ$.
    *   Wait, the dihedral angle is $60^\circ$. In this specific case (A is the projection point), the angle $\angle CAD$ *is* the dihedral angle (or related to it).
    *   Actually, if $C, D$ are in the plane perpendicular to $AB$ at $A$, then the angle $\angle CAD$ is exactly the angle between the projections, which is the dihedral angle $60^\circ$.
    *   So $\angle CAD = 60^\circ \neq 90^\circ$.
    *   Thus, $AC$ is not perpendicular to $AD$.
    *   **Option D is False**.

7.  **Re-evaluating everything.**
    *   Problem Statement Check: "A, B are two fixed points".
    *   "Moving point C distance to line AB is 2".
    *   "Moving point D distance to line AB is 1".
    *   "Dihedral angle C-AB-D is 60".
    *   The options are:
        A. $\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$
        B. $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$
        C. If $AB \perp CD$, then $CD \perp$ plane $ABD$.
        D. If $AB \perp$ plane $ACD$, then $AC \perp AD$.

    *   **Reviewing Option A**:
        *   We found $\cos(\angle CAD) = \frac{1+cd}{\sqrt{4+c^2}\sqrt{1+d^2}}$.
        *   We showed that if $c=d=k$ is large, $\cos \to 1$, angle $\to 0$.
        *   Wait, is there any constraint I missed?
        *   "A, B are fixed points". $C, D$ are moving points.
        *   Does "distance to line AB" restrict $C, D$ to be on a specific side? No.
        *   Does "Dihedral angle C-AB-D" restrict relative positions?
        *   Dihedral angle is usually defined in $[0, 180^\circ]$.
        *   If we consider the "opening" of the angle, it's 60.
        *   But $C$ and $D$ can be anywhere on the respective "cylinders" (lines distance 2 and 1 from axis).
        *   Wait, dihedral angle implies the relative position of the planes $ABC$ and $ABD$.
        *   Plane $ABC$ contains line $AB$ and point $C$.
        *   Plane $ABD$ contains line $AB$ and point $D$.
        *   The angle between these planes is $60^\circ$.
        *   This fixes the relative angular position of $C$ and $D$ around the axis.
        *   My coordinate setup $C=(1, \sqrt{3}, c)$ and $D=(1, 0, d)$ respects this. The angle between vector $(1, \sqrt{3})$ and $(1, 0)$ is 60.
        *   Wait, vector for $C$ in projection is $(1, \sqrt{3})$. Vector for $D$ is $(1, 0)$. Angle is 60.
        *   Wait, $(1, \sqrt{3})$ has angle 60 deg. $(1, 0)$ has angle 0 deg. Difference is 60.
        *   So the setup is valid.
        *   The $z$-coordinates $c$ and $d$ are independent?
        *   Yes, "Moving point C" means C can slide up and down the cylinder.
        *   So $c$ and $d$ can be anything.
        *   Therefore, my counterexample for A ($c=d \to \infty$) holds. Angle approaches 0. So A is False.
        *   *Correction*: Wait, $\angle CAD$ is the angle at vertex A.
        *   $A$ is at $(0,0,0)$.
        *   If $c, d \to \infty$, $C$ is at $(1, \sqrt{3}, \infty)$, $D$ is at $(1, 0, \infty)$.
        *   Vectors $\vec{AC}$ and $\vec{AD}$ both point almost straight up the $z$-axis.
        *   The angle between them approaches 0.
        *   So $\angle CAD$ can be very small.
        *   So $A$ is definitely False.

    *   **Reviewing Option B**:
        *   $CD^2 = 3 + (c-d)^2$.
        *   Min value is $\sqrt{3}$ when $c=d$.
        *   Is it possible for $c=d$?
        *   Yes, C and D can be at the same "height" (perpendicular plane to AB).
        *   So $B$ is True.

    *   **Reviewing Option C**:
        *   Condition: $AB \perp CD$.
        *   Vector $\vec{AB} \parallel (0,0,1)$.
        *   $\vec{CD} = (0, -\sqrt{3}, d-c)$.
        *   Dot product is $d-c$.
        *   So $AB \perp CD \iff c=d$.
        *   If $c=d$, $\vec{CD} = (0, -\sqrt{3}, 0)$.
        *   Plane $ABD$ contains $A(0,0,0)$, $B$ (on z-axis), $D(1,0,c)$.
        *   Points in plane $ABD$: $(x, 0, z)$. It is the $xz$-plane ($y=0$).
        *   Normal to plane $ABD$ is $(0,1,0)$.
        *   $\vec{CD}$ is parallel to $(0,1,0)$.
        *   So $CD \perp$ Plane $ABD$.
        *   Is this always true?
        *   Wait, what if $B$ coincides with $A$? Problem says "A, B are two fixed points". Implies distinct.
        *   What if $D$ lies in the plane $ABD$? It defines it.
        *   Is there any case where Plane $ABD$ is not well defined? If $A, B, D$ collinear.
        *   $A, B$ on $z$-axis. $D$ distance 1 from $z$-axis. So $D$ not on axis.
        *   So plane is unique.
        *   Logic seems solid. $C$ is True.

    *   **Reviewing Option D**:
        *   Condition: $AB \perp$ Plane $ACD$.
        *   Implies $AB \perp AC$ and $AB \perp AD$.
        *   In coordinates, $\vec{AC} = (1, \sqrt{3}, c)$, $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, d)$.
        *   $AB$ is along $z$.
        *   Dot products $\implies c=0, d=0$.
        *   So $C=(1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$, $D=(1, 0, 0)$.
        *   Check $AC \perp AD$.
        *   $\vec{AC} \cdot \vec{AD} = 1 \neq 0$.
        *   Angle is 60 degrees.
        *   So $AC$ not perpendicular to $AD$.
        *   D is False.

    *   **Wait, is there a trick with the Dihedral Angle definition?**
    *   "Dihedral angle C-AB-D".
    *   Usually defined by the angle between the half-planes originating from the edge AB.
    *   Could it be $120^\circ$? No, usually the smaller angle or specified. 60 is given.
    *   Does it constrain the $z$-coordinates? No.
    *   Does it constrain the relative position?
    *   Maybe I set up the coordinates such that the angle is 60, but maybe there are other configurations?
    *   Any configuration can be rotated to match mine.
    *   Is it possible that C and D are on opposite sides?
    *   If angle is 60, relative angle is 60.
    *   If I put $C$ at angle 0 and $D$ at 60?
    *   My setup: $D$ at 0 (on x-axis projection), $C$ at 60.
    *   Symmetry doesn't change distances or perpendicularity logic.

    *   **Let's double check Option A again.**
    *   Maybe I calculated $\angle CAD$ wrong.
    *   $A=(0,0,0)$.
    *   $C=(1, \sqrt{3}, c)$. $D=(1, 0, d)$.
    *   $\cos \theta = \frac{1+cd}{\sqrt{4+c^2}\sqrt{1+d^2}}$.
    *   We want to check if $\theta \ge 60^\circ \iff \cos \theta \le 1/2$.
    *   Is $\frac{1+cd}{\sqrt{4+c^2}\sqrt{1+d^2}} \le \frac{1}{2}$ always?
    *   Let's check $c=10, d=10$.
    *   Num = 101.
    *   Denom = $\sqrt{104} \cdot \sqrt{101} \approx 10.2 \cdot 10.05 \approx 102.5$.
    *   Ratio $\approx 101/102.5 \approx 0.98 > 0.5$.
    *   So $\cos \theta > 0.5$, so $\theta < 60^\circ$.
    *   Counterexample holds. A is False.

    *   **Let's double check Option C again.**
    *   "When $AB \perp CD$, $CD \perp$ plane $ABD$".
    *   My derivation: $AB \perp CD \implies c=d$.
    *   Then $CD$ vector is $(0, -\sqrt{3}, 0)$.
    *   Plane $ABD$ contains $z$-axis and point $(1,0,c)$.
    *   Equation of plane passing through $z$-axis and $(1,0,c)$?
    *   Wait. $A=(0,0,0)$, $B=(0,0,b)$, $D=(1,0,c)$.
    *   Points are $(0,0,0)$, $(0,0,1)$, $(1,0,0)$ essentially (scaling z).
    *   All have $y=0$.
    *   So plane is $y=0$.
    *   Normal is $(0,1,0)$.
    *   $CD$ vector is $(0, -\sqrt{3}, 0)$, which is parallel to normal.
    *   So $CD \perp$ Plane $ABD$.
    *   This seems correct.
    *   Wait, is "Plane ABD" unique? Yes.
    *   Is $CD$ a line or vector? Line segment.
    *   Yes.
    *   Is there any ambiguity?
    *   What if $A, B, D$ are not defining a plane? No, D is dist 1 from AB.
    *   What if $C$ is in the plane $ABD$?
    *   If $C$ is in plane $ABD$, then $C, D, A, B$ are coplanar.
    *   If $C$ is in $xz$-plane, its $y$-coord must be 0.
    *   But $C$'s projection distance is 2, angle 60.
    *   $y_C = 2 \sin 60 = \sqrt{3} \neq 0$.
    *   So $C$ is never in plane $ABD$ (unless dihedral angle was 0 or 180, but it's 60).
    *   So $CD$ is not in the plane.
    *   So it's a valid perpendicularity check.
    *   Conclusion: C is True.

    *   **Let's double check Option D again.**
    *   "When $AB \perp$ plane $ACD$, $AC \perp AD$".
    *   Condition: $AB \perp AC$ and $AB \perp AD$.
    *   In coordinates: $c=0, d=0$.
    *   This means $C$ and $D$ are in the plane passing through $A$ perpendicular to $AB$.
    *   Wait, $A$ is origin. Plane perpendicular to $AB$ ($z$-axis) at $A$ is $xy$-plane ($z=0$).
    *   So $C$ and $D$ are in $xy$-plane.
    *   Distance of $C$ to $z$-axis is 2. So $C$ is on circle radius 2.
    *   Distance of $D$ to $z$-axis is 1. So $D$ is on circle radius 1.
    *   Dihedral angle is angle between plane $ABC$ and $ABD$.
    *   Plane $ABC$ contains $z$-axis and $C$. Since $C$ in $xy$-plane, plane $ABC$ is the plane spanned by $z$-axis and vector $\vec{OC}$.
    *   Plane $ABD$ contains $z$-axis and $D$. Spanned by $z$-axis and $\vec{OD}$.
    *   The angle between these planes is the angle between $\vec{OC}$ and $\vec{OD}$ in $xy$-plane.
    *   This angle is given as $60^\circ$.
    *   So $\angle COD = 60^\circ$.
    *   Triangle $ACD$? No, points $A, C, D$.
    *   $A$ is origin $O$.
    *   So we are looking at angle $\angle CAD = \angle COD = 60^\circ$.
    *   Question asks: Is $AC \perp AD$?
    *   i.e., Is $\angle CAD = 90^\circ$?
    *   No, it is $60^\circ$.
    *   So $AC$ is not perpendicular to $AD$.
    *   D is False.

    *   **Is there any interpretation where D could be true?**
    *   "AB perpendicular to plane ACD".
    *   This implies $AB \perp AC$ and $AB \perp AD$.
    *   Wait, $A$ is on line $AB$.
    *   So $AC$ and $AD$ must be perpendicular to $AB$ at $A$.
    *   This means $C$ and $D$ must lie in the plane perpendicular to $AB$ at $A$.
    *   This forces the geometry I analyzed.
    *   The angle between $AC$ and $AD$ is determined by the dihedral angle only if $C, D$ are in that perpendicular plane?
    *   Actually, yes. If $C, D$ are in the plane $\perp AB$ at $A$, the angle $\angle CAD$ is the angle between the vectors $\vec{AC}$ and $\vec{AD}$.
    *   The dihedral angle is the angle between the planes containing these vectors and the line $AB$.
    *   Since the vectors are perpendicular to the line, the angle between the vectors *is* the dihedral angle.
    *   So $\angle CAD = 60^\circ$.
    *   So $AC \not\perp AD$.
    *   So D is definitely False.

    *   **Is there any interpretation where B could be false?**
    *   $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$.
    *   Distance formula $CD = \sqrt{3 + (c-d)^2}$.
    *   Is it possible that $(c-d)^2 < 0$? No.
    *   Is it possible that the projection distance is not $\sqrt{3}$?
    *   Let's re-verify projection distance.
    *   Let line $AB$ be $z$-axis.
    *   $C = (x_C, y_C, z_C)$ with $x_C^2 + y_C^2 = 4$.
    *   $D = (x_D, y_D, z_D)$ with $x_D^2 + y_D^2 = 1$.
    *   Angle between vector $(x_C, y_C)$ and $(x_D, y_D)$ is $60^\circ$.
    *   Distance squared between projections $C'(x_C, y_C, 0)$ and $D'(x_D, y_D, 0)$:
    *   $|C'D'|^2 = |C'|^2 + |D'|^2 - 2|C'||D'|\cos(60^\circ)$
    *   $= 2^2 + 1^2 - 2(2)(1)(1/2) = 4 + 1 - 2 = 3$.
    *   So horizontal distance is $\sqrt{3}$.
    *   3D distance $CD = \sqrt{|C'D'|^2 + (z_C - z_D)^2} = \sqrt{3 + (z_C - z_D)^2}$.
    *   Minimum is $\sqrt{3}$.
    *   So B is always True.

    *   **Is there any interpretation where C could be false?**
    *   Condition: $AB \perp CD$.
    *   Vector $\vec{AB} \parallel \vec{k}$.
    *   $\vec{CD} = (x_D-x_C, y_D-y_C, z_D-z_C)$.
    *   $\vec{AB} \cdot \vec{CD} = z_D - z_C = 0 \implies z_C = z_D$.
    *   So $C, D$ have same $z$.
    *   $C' = C, D' = D$ (in terms of x,y coords relative to z).
    *   $C = (2\cos\alpha, 2\sin\alpha, z)$, $D = (\cos\alpha, \sin\alpha, z)$? No, angle diff is 60.
    *   Let's use polar.
    *   $D = (1, 0, z)$ (rotated frame).
    *   $C = (2\cos 60, 2\sin 60, z) = (1, \sqrt{3}, z)$.
    *   $\vec{CD} = (1-1, 0-\sqrt{3}, z-z) = (0, -\sqrt{3}, 0)$.
    *   Plane $ABD$. Points $A(0,0,0)$, $B(0,0,b)$, $D(1,0,z)$.
    *   Wait, if $A$ is origin, $B$ is $(0,0,b)$.
    *   $D$ is $(1,0,z)$.
    *   Vectors in plane: $\vec{AB} = (0,0,b)$, $\vec{AD} = (1,0,z)$.
    *   Normal vector $\vec{n} = \vec{AB} \times \vec{AD}$.
    *   $\vec{n} = \begin{vmatrix} \mathbf{i} & \mathbf{j} & \mathbf{k} \\ 0 & 0 & b \\ 1 & 0 & z \end{vmatrix} = \mathbf{i}(0) - \mathbf{j}(-b) + \mathbf{k}(0) = (0, b, 0)$.
    *   So normal is along $y$-axis $(0,1,0)$.
    *   Vector $\vec{CD} = (0, -\sqrt{3}, 0)$ is parallel to $y$-axis.
    *   So $\vec{CD}$ is parallel to normal $\vec{n}$.
    *   So $CD \perp$ Plane $ABD$.
    *   This holds regardless of $z$ (position of C, D along line) as long as $z_C=z_D$.
    *   Wait, $D$ coordinates were $(1, 0, z)$. $A$ is $(0,0,0)$.
    *   Does the plane $ABD$ change if $D$ moves in $z$?
    *   $A=(0,0,0)$, $B=(0,0,b)$, $D=(1,0,z)$.
    *   Equation of plane passing through $z$-axis?
    *   Since $A, B$ are on $z$-axis, the plane must contain the $z$-axis.
    *   A plane containing the $z$-axis has equation $ax + by = 0$.
    *   Since $D(1,0,z)$ is in it: $a(1) + b(0) = 0 \implies a=0$.
    *   So equation is $by = 0 \implies y=0$ (assuming $b \ne 0$).
    *   Wait, if $b=0$, plane is undefined (points collinear), but $B$ is distinct from $A$? "Two fixed points". Usually implies distinct. Even if $B=A$, "line AB" is not defined, but problem says "distance to line AB". So line exists. So $A \ne B$.
    *   So plane $ABD$ is indeed $y=0$.
    *   Wait, is $D$ always at $y=0$?
    *   We rotated the system so $D$ is at $y=0$.
    *   In that system, $C$ is at $y=\sqrt{3}$ (since angle 60, radius 2).
    *   So $CD$ vector has $\Delta y = -\sqrt{3}$, $\Delta x = 0$, $\Delta z = 0$.
    *   So $CD$ is parallel to $y$-axis.
    *   Plane $ABD$ is $xz$-plane ($y=0$).
    *   So $CD \perp$ Plane $ABD$.
    *   Yes. C is True.

    *   **Let's check if there are any "gotchas" with the definition of Dihedral Angle.**
    *   Dihedral angle $C-AB-D$.
    *   Edges are rays $AC$ and $AD$? No, edge is line $AB$. Faces are planes $ABC$ and $ABD$.
    *   Angle is measured in a plane perpendicular to the edge $AB$.
    *   My analysis used exactly this.
    *   The angle between the normal vectors of the faces? No, angle between the faces.
    *   Or angle between lines in the faces perpendicular to the edge.
    *   The lines perpendicular to $AB$ passing through intersection point?
    *   Actually, any plane perpendicular to $AB$ cuts the faces in lines. The angle between these lines is the dihedral angle.
    *   My projection method uses exactly these lines (radius vectors in the cross-section).
    *   So the geometry is correct.

    *   **Let's check Option A again just in case.**
    *   $\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$.
    *   We found a counter example where $\angle CAD \to 0$.
    *   Is there any constraint I missed?
    *   "Moving point C distance to line AB is 2".
    *   Maybe C is constrained to a specific plane? No, "In space".
    *   Maybe A and B are specific? "Fixed points".
    *   Maybe distance is segment distance? "Distance to line AB". Standard definition is perpendicular distance.
    *   Maybe "Dihedral angle C-AB-D" implies C and D are on specific sides?
    *   Usually dihedral angle is the inner angle, $\le 180$.
    *   If it's 60, it's 60.
    *   Maybe $C$ and $D$ must be on the same side of the plane perpendicular to AB passing through A?
    *   No, distance is to the infinite line.
    *   So $z$ can be anything.
    *   Wait, if $z_C$ and $z_D$ are very far from $A$ (origin) but close to each other?
    *   Example: $A=(0,0,0)$.
    *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, 1000)$.
    *   $D = (1, 0, 1000)$.
    *   $C, D$ distance to $z$-axis are 2, 1.
    *   Angle between planes is 60.
    *   $\vec{AC} = (1, \sqrt{3}, 1000)$.
    *   $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, 1000)$.
    *   Dot product $\approx 10^6$.
    *   Lengths $\approx 1000$.
    *   Cosine $\approx 1$. Angle $\approx 0$.
    *   Is it possible that $C$ and $D$ cannot be at $z=1000$?
    *   Why not? "Moving point".
    *   Maybe the problem implies a tetrahedron $ABCD$? No, just points.
    *   Is it possible that $A$ is not on the line segment between projections?
    *   $A$ is a fixed point. $C$ moves.
    *   Wait, is $A$ the origin of the coordinate system? Yes, arbitrarily chosen.
    *   Does the position of $A$ on the line matter?
    *   Yes.
    *   In my counter example, $A$ is at $z=0$, $C, D$ at $z=1000$.
    *   Is this allowed?
    *   $C$ distance to line $AB$ is 2. Yes.
    *   $D$ distance to line $AB$ is 1. Yes.
    *   Dihedral angle 60. Yes.
    *   $A$ is a fixed point. Yes.
    *   So this configuration is valid.
    *   Thus A is False.

    *   **Let's check Option B again.**
    *   Is it possible $CD < \sqrt{3}$?
    *   We proved $CD^2 = 3 + (z_C - z_D)^2$.
    *   This relied on the projection distance being $\sqrt{3}$.
    *   Projection distance depends on $r_C=2, r_D=1$ and angle $60^\circ$.
    *   Law of cosines: $d_{proj}^2 = 2^2 + 1^2 - 2(2)(1)\cos 60 = 5 - 2 = 3$.
    *   This is correct.
    *   So $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$ is solid.

    *   **Let's check Option C again.**
    *   "When $AB \perp CD$".
    *   This means vector $CD$ is orthogonal to vector $AB$.
    *   $AB$ is direction of line.
    *   So $CD$ must be perpendicular to the axis.
    *   This implies $C$ and $D$ have the same projection onto the line $AB$?
    *   Let $P_C, P_D$ be projections of $C, D$ onto line $AB$.
    *   $\vec{CD} = \vec{P_C P_D} + \vec{C' D'}$? No.
    *   Decompose $\vec{CD}$ into component parallel to $AB$ and perpendicular to $AB$.
    *   $\vec{CD}_{\parallel} = (\text{proj of } D - \text{proj of } C)$.
    *   $\vec{CD}_{\perp} = \vec{C'D'}$ (vector between projections in perp plane).
    *   If $AB \perp CD$, then $\vec{CD}_{\parallel} = 0$.
    *   So projection of $C$ and $D$ onto line $AB$ coincide.
    *   So $C, D$ are in a plane perpendicular to $AB$.
    *   In this plane, $A$ (projection of A? No A is on line) is a point on the line.
    *   Wait, $A$ is on the line $AB$. So $A$ is the intersection of line $AB$ and this perpendicular plane?
    *   No. The plane perpendicular to $AB$ containing $C$ and $D$ intersects $AB$ at some point $P$.
    *   $P$ is the projection of $C$ and $D$.
    *   $A$ is a fixed point on $AB$. $A$ might not be $P$.
    *   So $A, C, D$ form a triangle.
    *   $AC$ and $AD$ are segments from $A$ to $C$ and $D$.
    *   $C, D$ are in plane $\pi \perp AB$ at $P$.
    *   Distance $PC = 2$, $PD = 1$. Angle $\angle CPD = 60^\circ$?
    *   Wait, dihedral angle is angle between plane $ABC$ and $ABD$.
    *   Plane $ABC$ contains line $AB$ and $C$.
    *   Since $AB \perp \pi$, the intersection of Plane $ABC$ and $\pi$ is the line $PC$.
    *   Similarly intersection of Plane $ABD$ and $\pi$ is line $PD$.
    *   The angle between planes is the angle between lines $PC$ and $PD$ in $\pi$.
    *   So $\angle CPD = 60^\circ$.
    *   So in $\triangle PCD$, $PC=2, PD=1, \angle P=60^\circ$.
    *   $CD = \sqrt{3}$ (calculated before).
    *   Now, consider the statement: $CD \perp$ Plane $ABD$.
    *   Plane $ABD$ contains line $AB$ and point $D$.
    *   Since $AB \perp \pi$ and $D \in \pi$, Plane $ABD$ is perpendicular to $\pi$?
    *   Yes, a plane containing a line perpendicular to another plane is perpendicular to that plane.
    *   Line $AB$ is in Plane $ABD$ and $AB \perp \pi$.
    *   So Plane $ABD \perp \pi$.
    *   The intersection of Plane $ABD$ and $\pi$ is the line $PD$.
    *   For a line $CD$ (which lies in $\pi$) to be perpendicular to Plane $ABD$, it must be perpendicular to the intersection line $PD$?
    *   Wait. Theorem: If two planes are perpendicular, a line in one plane is perpendicular to the other plane if and only if it is perpendicular to the intersection line.
    *   Is this true?
    *   Let planes be $\alpha, \beta$ with $\alpha \perp \beta$. Intersection line $L$.
    *   Line $m \subset \alpha$. $m \perp \beta \iff m \perp L$.
    *   Proof: If $m \perp \beta$, $m$ is perp to every line in $\beta$ through intersection. So $m \perp L$.
    *   Conversely, if $m \perp L$, and $m \subset \alpha \perp \beta$?
    *   Normal to $\beta$ lies in $\alpha$ and is perp to $L$. So $m$ must be parallel to normal.
    *   Yes.
    *   So, is $CD \perp PD$?
    *   In $\triangle PCD$:
    *   Sides $PC=2, PD=1, CD=\sqrt{3}$.
    *   Check Pythagoras: $1^2 + (\sqrt{3})^2 = 1 + 3 = 4 = 2^2$.
    *   So $PD^2 + CD^2 = PC^2$.
    *   This implies $\angle PDC = 90^\circ$.
    *   So $CD \perp PD$.
    *   Since $CD \subset \pi$, $PD \subset \pi$, and Plane $ABD \perp \pi$ with intersection $PD$.
    *   And $CD \perp PD$.
    *   Therefore $CD \perp$ Plane $ABD$.
    *   This confirms Option C is True using pure geometry without coordinates.
    *   Very robust.

    *   **Let's re-check Option D with geometry.**
    *   "When $AB \perp$ Plane $ACD$".
    *   This implies $AB \perp AC$ and $AB \perp AD$.
    *   So $AC \perp AB$ and $AD \perp AB$.
    *   This means $C$ and $D$ lie in the plane passing through $A$ and perpendicular to $AB$.
    *   Let this plane be $\sigma$. $A \in \sigma$.
    *   Since $C \in \sigma$, distance from $C$ to line $AB$ is just distance $AC$.
    *   Wait. Distance from point to line.
    *   If $C$ is in plane $\perp AB$ at $A$, then the perpendicular from $C$ to $AB$ lands on $A$.
    *   So distance is $AC$.
    *   Given distance is 2. So $AC = 2$.
    *   Similarly, $D \in \sigma$, distance to $AB$ is $AD = 1$.
    *   So in plane $\sigma$, we have $A$ as center. $C$ is at distance 2, $D$ at distance 1.
    *   The dihedral angle $C-AB-D$ is the angle between plane $ABC$ and $ABD$.
    *   Plane $ABC$ contains $AB$ and $C$. Since $AC \perp AB$, the plane is determined by $AB$ and $AC$.
    *   Plane $ABD$ contains $AB$ and $D$.
    *   The angle between these planes is the angle between lines $AC$ and $AD$ (since both are $\perp AB$ at $A$).
    *   So $\angle CAD = 60^\circ$.
    *   Option D claims $AC \perp AD$ (i.e. $\angle CAD = 90^\circ$).
    *   Contradiction ($60 \ne 90$).
    *   So D is False.

    *   **Re-check Option A with geometry.**
    *   $\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$.
    *   We found a case where $\angle CAD$ is small.
    *   Geometric view:
    *   $A$ is on line $AB$.
    *   $C$ is distance 2 from line. $D$ is distance 1 from line.
    *   Angle between planes is 60.
    *   If $C, D$ are very far from $A$ along the line direction (large $z$), the vectors $\vec{AC}$ and $\vec{AD}$ become almost parallel to $AB$.
    *   The angle between them becomes small.
    *   So A is False.

    *   **Re-check Option B with geometry.**
    *   $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$.
    *   Let $P_C, P_D$ be projections of $C, D$ on line $AB$.
    *   $C P_C = 2, D P_D = 1$.
    *   $C P_C \perp AB, D P_D \perp AB$.
    *   Angle between planes is angle between $C P_C$ and $D P_D$?
    *   Not exactly. The angle is between the planes containing these perpendiculars and the line.
    *   Wait, $C P_C$ is a line segment perpendicular to $AB$. $D P_D$ is too.
    *   Translate $D P_D$ to start at $P_C$. Let it be $D' P_C$.
    *   Angle between $C P_C$ and $D' P_C$ is the dihedral angle $60^\circ$.
    *   Distance $C D'$ (in the plane perpendicular to AB at $P_C$) is $\sqrt{2^2 + 1^2 - 2(2)(1)\cos 60} = \sqrt{3}$.
    *   The distance $CD$ in 3D is hypotenuse of right triangle with legs $CD'$ and $P_C P_D$ (distance along line).
    *   $CD^2 = (CD')^2 + (P_C P_D)^2 = 3 + (P_C P_D)^2$.
    *   So $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$.
    *   True.

    *   **Conclusion:**
    *   A: False.
    *   B: True.
    *   C: True.
    *   D: False.

    *   Wait, is it a multiple choice question where multiple answers can be correct?
    *   "多选题" means Multiple Choice (Select all that apply).
    *   So B and C should be the answers.

    *   **Let's double check if I missed any subtleties.**
    *   "A, B fixed points".
    *   Maybe $A$ and $B$ distance matters?
    *   In my analysis, I set $A$ at origin. $B$ at $(0,0,b)$.
    *   If $b=0$, $A=B$, line is not defined. But "line AB" implies $A \ne B$.
    *   If $A, B$ are very close? No, line is infinite.
    *   Are there any constraints on C, D relative to segment AB?
    *   No, "distance to line AB".
    *   Is it possible that $C$ and $D$ are on the same side of the plane perpendicular to $AB$ through $A$?
    *   Or opposite sides?
    *   Does not affect distance $CD$ or perpendicularity logic.

    *   **Let's check Option C again for "Edge Cases".**
    *   "When $AB \perp CD$".
    *   This implies $P_C = P_D$. Let's call it $P$.
    *   $C, D, P$ form a triangle in plane $\perp AB$.
    *   $PC=2, PD=1, \angle CPD = 60$.
    *   $CD = \sqrt{3}$.
    *   We proved $CD \perp PD$ because $1^2 + (\sqrt{3})^2 = 2^2$.
    *   So $CD \perp PD$.
    *   Plane $ABD$ contains line $AB$ and point $D$.
    *   Since $AB \perp$ plane $PCD$, line $AB \perp CD$. (This is given).
    *   Also $AB \perp PD$ (since $PD$ in plane $PCD$).
    *   So $AB$ is perpendicular to two intersecting lines $CD$ and $PD$ in plane $ABD$?
    *   Wait. $CD$ and $PD$ intersect at $D$.
    *   Are they in plane $ABD$?
    *   $PD$ is in plane $ABD$ (contains $D$ and $P$? $P$ is on $AB$, so yes).
    *   $CD$? No. $C$ is not in plane $ABD$ generally.
    *   We want to show $CD \perp$ Plane $ABD$.
    *   We know $CD \perp AB$ (given).
    *   We know $CD \perp PD$ (from geometry of $\triangle PCD$).
    *   $AB$ and $PD$ are two intersecting lines in Plane $ABD$?
    *   $AB$ passes through $P$. $PD$ passes through $P$?
    *   Wait, $P$ is on $AB$. $D$ is in plane. So $PD$ connects a point on line to a point in plane. Yes, $PD \subset$ Plane $ABD$.
    *   Do $AB$ and $PD$ intersect? Yes at $P$.
    *   Are they distinct?
    *   $PD$ has length 1. $D$ is not on $AB$ (distance 1). So $PD$ is not part of $AB$.
    *   So $AB$ and $PD$ define the plane $ABD$.
    *   Since $CD$ is perpendicular to both $AB$ and $PD$, $CD$ is perpendicular to the plane defined by them, which is $ABD$.
    *   Logic holds perfectly.

    *   **Let's check Option D again.**
    *   "When $AB \perp$ Plane $ACD$".
    *   This means $AB$ is normal to plane $ACD$.
    *   So $AB \perp AC$ and $AB \perp AD$.
    *   This implies $C$ and $D$ project onto $A$ on the line $AB$.
    *   So $P_C = A$ and $P_D = A$.
    *   So $P_C = P_D = A$.
    *   This is a specific case of the previous scenario where $P$ coincides with $A$.
    *   In this case, we are looking at $\triangle ACD$ in the plane perpendicular to $AB$ at $A$.
    *   $AC = 2, AD = 1, \angle CAD = 60^\circ$.
    *   Is $AC \perp AD$?
    *   No, angle is 60.
    *   So D is False.
    *   Wait, could $\angle CAD$ be something else?
    *   Dihedral angle is 60.
    *   If $C, D$ project to $A$, the angle between $AC$ and $AD$ *is* the dihedral angle.
    *   Is it possible the dihedral angle is defined differently?
    *   Dihedral angle between planes $ABC$ and $ABD$.
    *   If $AC \perp AB$ and $AD \perp AB$, the angle between planes is angle between $AC$ and $AD$.
    *   Yes.
    *   So D is strictly False.

    *   **Are there any trick interpretations?**
    *   Maybe $C-AB-D$ notation implies order?
    *   No, standard notation for dihedral angle.
    *   Maybe distance is to segment $AB$?
    *   "Distance to line AB" (直线AB). In Chinese math problems, "直线" is line, "线段" is segment. It says 直线.
    *   So infinite line.

    *   **Let's check Option A again.**
    *   Maybe I calculated $\cos \theta$ wrong?
    *   $\cos \theta = \frac{1+cd}{\sqrt{4+c^2}\sqrt{1+d^2}}$.
    *   We want to check if $\theta \ge 60 \iff \cos \theta \le 0.5$.
    *   Inequality: $\frac{1+cd}{\sqrt{4+c^2}\sqrt{1+d^2}} \le \frac{1}{2}$.
    *   Square both sides (assuming LHS positive, which it is if $cd \ge -1$).
    *   $4(1+cd)^2 \le (4+c^2)(1+d^2) = 4 + 4d^2 + c^2 + c^2d^2$.
    *   $4(1 + 2cd + c^2d^2) \le 4 + 4d^2 + c^2 + c^2d^2$.
    *   $4 + 8cd + 4c^2d^2 \le 4 + 4d^2 + c^2 + c^2d^2$.
    *   $3c^2d^2 + 8cd - 4d^2 - c^2 \le 0$.
    *   Let's check if this is always true.
    *   Test $c=d=10$.
    *   $3(10000) + 8(100) - 4(100) - 100 = 30000 + 800 - 400 - 100 = 30300 > 0$.
    *   So inequality fails.
    *   So $\cos \theta > 0.5$.
    *   So $\theta < 60$.
    *   Confirmed A is False.

    *   **What if $c, d$ have different signs?**
    *   Say $c=10, d=-10$.
    *   $cd = -100$.
    *   Num = $1 - 100 = -99$.
    *   $\cos \theta$ is negative.
    *   Angle $> 90$.
    *   $90 > 60$.
    *   So in this case A holds.
    *   But A says $\ge 60$ for *all* configurations (implied by "则" - "then").
    *   Usually implies "for all valid C, D".
    *   Since we found a counter example where it's false, the statement A is false.

    *   **Wait, is "则" (then) implying a conditional or a universal quantifier?**
    *   "若 ... 则 ..." (If ..., then ...).
    *   The "If" part is the setup: A, B fixed, C dist 2, D dist 1, Dihedral 60.
    *   This setup defines a locus of possible configurations.
    *   The options are statements that must hold true for *any* configuration satisfying the setup.
    *   So yes, universal quantifier.

    *   **Summary:**
    *   A: False.
    *   B: True.
    *   C: True.
    *   D: False.

    *   **Let's do a quick sanity check on the text of the options.**
    *   A. $\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$
    *   B. $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$
    *   C. 当 $AB \perp CD$ 时， $CD \perp$ 平面 $ABD$
    *   D. 当 $AB \perp$ 平面 $ACD$ 时， $AC \perp AD$

    *   Everything seems consistent.

    *   **One final check on Option C.**
    *   "When $AB \perp CD$".
    *   Is it possible for $AB \perp CD$ to happen?
    *   Yes, if $z_C = z_D$.
    *   Is it possible for $CD \perp$ plane $ABD$ to happen?
    *   Yes, we proved it happens exactly when $AB \perp CD$.
    *   Wait, is it "If P then Q" or "P iff Q"?
    *   Statement: "When P, Q". Means $P \implies Q$.
    *   We proved $P \implies Q$.
    *   Is it possible that $Q$ is true but $P$ is false? Maybe, but irrelevant.
    *   Is it possible that $P$ is true but $Q$ is false?
    *   We proved if $P$ ($c=d$), then $Q$ ($CD \perp ABD$).
    *   Wait, did I assume $A$ is origin?
    *   Yes. But geometry is translation invariant along the line.
    *   The relative positions matter.
    *   If $A$ is far away, does it change?
    *   Plane $ABD$ passes through $A$ and $B$ (line) and $D$.
    *   So Plane $ABD$ is the plane containing line $AB$ and point $D$.
    *   This plane is fixed by the choice of $D$ (angular position) and the line $AB$.
    *   It does *not* depend on $A$'s specific position on the line, only that $A$ is on the line.
    *   Wait, if $A$ is on the line, the plane containing the line and $D$ is unique.
    *   Yes.
    *   So the plane $ABD$ is well defined.
    *   My proof used $A$ as origin to define coordinates, but the plane $ABD$ was $y=0$ (containing $z$-axis and $D(1,0,z)$).
    *   This plane contains the whole $z$-axis (line $AB$).
    *   So the proof holds for any position of $A$ on the line.
    *   So C is robust.

    *   **One final check on Option B.**
    *   $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$.
    *   We derived $CD^2 = 3 + (z_C - z_D)^2$.
    *   Is it possible that the distance between projections is not $\sqrt{3}$?
    *   Distance between two lines parallel to $AB$? No.
    *   Distance between point $C$ and point $D$.
    *   Projections $C', D'$ on a plane $\perp AB$.
    *   Distance $C'D' = \sqrt{r_C^2 + r_D^2 - 2 r_C r_D \cos \theta}$.
    *   $r_C=2, r_D=1, \theta=60$.
    *   $d^2 = 4 + 1 - 2(2)(1)(0.5) = 3$.
    *   $d = \sqrt{3}$.
    *   Distance $CD = \sqrt{d^2 + \Delta z^2} \ge \sqrt{3}$.
    *   Robust.

    *   **Is there any constraint on $C, D$ being distinct?**
    *   Distance 2 vs 1. They can never coincide.
    *   So $CD > 0$.
    *   $\sqrt{3} \approx 1.732$.

    *   **What if the dihedral angle was measured differently?**
    *   "Dihedral angle C-AB-D".
    *   Usually means the angle between the half-planes.
    *   Range $(0, 180)$.
    *   If it was 120, $\cos 120 = -0.5$.
    *   Then $d_{proj}^2 = 4+1 - 2(2)(1)(-0.5) = 5+2=7$.
    *   Then $CD \ge \sqrt{7}$.
    *   But it is 60.
    *   Also $\angle CAD$ analysis would change.
    *   But problem states 60.

    *   **Let's check if $A$ could be "between" $C$ and $D$ in some projection sense?**
    *   Doesn't matter.

    *   **Let's write down the steps for the solution.**
    *   1. Establish coordinate system. Line AB as z-axis. A at origin (or arbitrary point on axis).
    *   2. Define coordinates of C and D based on distance and dihedral angle.
       $C = (2\cos\alpha, 2\sin\alpha, z_C)$, $D = (2\cos(\alpha+60^\circ), 1\sin(\alpha+60^\circ), z_D)$?
       Simpler: Rotate so D is in xz-plane.
       $D = (1, 0, z_D)$.
       $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_C)$. (Since angle 60, radius 2).
    *   3. Analyze A. Calculate $\cos \angle CAD$. Show it can be $> 0.5$ (angle $< 60$).
    *   4. Analyze B. Calculate distance $CD$. Show min value is $\sqrt{3}$.
    *   5. Analyze C. Condition $AB \perp CD \implies z_C = z_D$. Check normal of plane ABD. Show parallel to CD.
    *   6. Analyze D. Condition $AB \perp$ Plane ACD $\implies z_C = z_D = z_A = 0$ (assuming A at origin). Check dot product of AC and AD. Show non-zero.

    *   **Wait, for D, $A$ is at origin $(0,0,0)$.**
    *   If $AB \perp$ Plane $ACD$, then $AB \perp AC$ and $AB \perp AD$.
    *   $\vec{AB} = (0,0,1)$.
    *   $\vec{AC} = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_C)$. Dot product $z_C = 0$.
    *   $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, z_D)$. Dot product $z_D = 0$.
    *   So $C=(1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$, $D=(1, 0, 0)$.
    *   $\vec{AC} \cdot \vec{AD} = 1$. Not 0.
    *   So D is false.
    *   Wait, is it possible $A$ is not origin?
    *   If $A$ is at $(0,0, a)$.
    *   $\vec{AC} = C - A = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_C - a)$.
    *   $\vec{AD} = D - A = (1, 0, z_D - a)$.
    *   $AB$ is along z-axis. Direction $(0,0,1)$.
    *   $AB \perp AC \implies z_C - a = 0 \implies z_C = a$.
    *   $AB \perp AD \implies z_D - a = 0 \implies z_D = a$.
    *   So $C, D$ are in plane $z=a$ passing through $A$.
    *   Coordinates in that plane (shifted to A): $C'=(1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$, $D'=(1, 0, 0)$.
    *   Vectors from A are same.
    *   Dot product is 1.
    *   Result holds.

    *   **Double check Option C with $A$ at $(0,0,a)$.**
    *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_C)$, $D = (1, 0, z_D)$.
    *   $AB$ direction $(0,0,1)$.
    *   $AB \perp CD \implies \vec{CD} \cdot (0,0,1) = 0 \implies z_D - z_C = 0 \implies z_C = z_D = z$.
    *   Plane $ABD$. Points $A(0,0,a)$, $B$ (any point on z-axis), $D(1,0,z)$.
    *   Vector $\vec{AB}$ is along z-axis.
    *   Vector $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, z-a)$.
    *   Normal to plane $ABD$ = $\vec{AB} \times \vec{AD}$.
    *   $(0,0,1) \times (1, 0, z-a) = \det \dots = (0, 1, 0)$.
    *   Vector $\vec{CD} = D - C = (1-1, 0-\sqrt{3}, z-z) = (0, -\sqrt{3}, 0)$.
    *   $\vec{CD}$ is parallel to $(0,1,0)$.
    *   So $\vec{CD}$ is parallel to normal.
    *   So $CD \perp$ Plane $ABD$.
    *   Holds.

    *   **Are there any constraints on $B$?**
    *   $B$ is a fixed point.
    *   If $B=A$, line is undefined.
    *   If $B$ is distinct, line is defined.
    *   The problem implies line AB exists.
    *   So everything is fine.

    *   **Final Answer Selection.**
    *   Options B and C.

    *   Let's check the wording of the question again to ensure no misinterpretation.
    *   "多选题" (Multiple choice question with multiple correct answers).
    *   "A, B为两个定点" (A, B are two fixed points).
    *   "动点C到直线AB的距离为2" (Moving point C distance to line AB is 2).
    *   "动点D到直线AB的距离为1" (Moving point D distance to line AB is 1).
    *   "二面角C - AB - D为60°" (Dihedral angle C-AB-D is 60 degrees).
    *   "则" (Then/So).
    *   A. $\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$
    *   B. $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$
    *   C. 当$AB \perp CD$时，$CD \perp$平面$ABD$ (When AB perp CD, CD perp Plane ABD)
    *   D. 当$AB \perp$平面$ACD$时，$AC \perp AD$ (When AB perp Plane ACD, AC perp AD)

    *   Looks solid.

    *   **One detail on Option A.**
    *   Is it possible that $\angle CAD$ is always $\ge 60$ if we consider the geometry in a specific way?
    *   Maybe $A$ is the "vertex" of the angle.
    *   We established $A$ is just a point on the line.
    *   If $C, D$ are "above" A, angle is small.
    *   If $C, D$ are "below" A, angle is small.
    *   If one above, one below?
    *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, 10)$, $D = (1, 0, -10)$.
    *   $\vec{AC} = (1, \sqrt{3}, 10)$, $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, -10)$.
    *   Dot prod = $1 - 100 = -99$.
    *   Lengths $\approx 10, 10$.
    *   $\cos \theta \approx -1$.
    *   Angle $\approx 180$.
    *   $180 \ge 60$. True.
    *   But the question asks if the statement is *always* true.
    *   Since we found cases where it's false (small angle), the statement is false.
    *   Wait, is it possible that $C$ and $D$ cannot be on the same side of $A$?
    *   No restriction.
    *   So A is False.

    *   **Is it possible that B is false?**
    *   $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$.
    *   We found minimum is $\sqrt{3}$.
    *   Is it possible to have distance less?
    *   Distance squared = $3 + (z_C - z_D)^2$.
    *   Always $\ge 3$.
    *   So B is True.

    *   **Is it possible that C is false?**
    *   We proved it's true.
    *   Is there any degenerate case?
    *   $C=D$? Impossible (dist 2 vs 1).
    *   $AB$ parallel to $CD$? Then not perpendicular. Condition "When AB perp CD" is not met. Implication is vacuously true or not applicable. But if met, conclusion holds.
    *   Wait, implication "If P then Q". If P is impossible, statement is true.
    *   But P is possible.
    *   So we checked the case where P is true. Q is true.
    *   So statement is True.

    *   **Is it possible that D is false?**
    *   We proved it's false.
    *   If $AB \perp$ Plane $ACD$, then $AC \not\perp AD$.
    *   So "If P then Q" is False because P is possible and Q is false.
    *   So D is False.

    *   **Result:** B, C.
